On the geometric Satake equivalence for Kac-Moody groups
Alexis Bouthier and Eric Vasserot

Abstract
. This article establishes a geometric Satake equivalence for affine Kac-Moody groups as an equivalence of abelian semisimple categories over algebraically closed fields. We define a well-behaved category of equivariant sheaves on the ∞ \infty -stack Gr G \Gr_{G} that we equip with a t t -structure. We obtain an Braden’s hyperbolic localization theorem for such a stack and prove that the constant term functor is t t -exact using dimension estimates for affine MV-cycles. We then deduce the sought-for equivalence and prove that the IC \IC -complexes match with the irreducible highest weight representations of the dual group G ∨ G^{\vee} .



In memoriam Gérard Laumon (1952-2025)

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Motivations and brief outline

Let
G
be a connected reductive group.
The affine Grassmannian
\Gr_{G}=G(\!(s)\!)/G\llbracket s\rrbracket

has a natural stratification by
G\llbracket s\rrbracket
-orbits indexed by dominant cocharacters
\lambda
.
The geometric Satake equivalence for
G
relates the
\IC
-complex of the closure

\overline{\Gr}_{\lambda}
of
\Gr_{\lambda}=G\llbracket s\rrbracket\cdot s^{\lambda}
in
\Gr_{G}

with the irreducible representation
L(\lambda)
of highest weight
\lambda
of the Langlands
dual group
G^{\vee}
. It has been initiated by Lusztig in
[
63
]
and culminates with the work of Mirkovic-Vilonen
[
71
]
with
contributions of Ginzburg
[
41
]
and Beilinson-Drinfeld
[
5
]
.

The search for an analog for Kac-Moody groups was initiated by I. Frenkel and I. Grojnowski 20 years ago
and has since then been an active open problem. Because of its highly infinite nature and the lack of technology to
treat such spaces, the current approach, initiated by Braverman-Finkelberg in
[
12
]
,
[
13
]
and
[
14
]
,
is to formulate a Satake equivalence in terms on some analog of finite dimensional transversal slices to orbits in the affine Grassmannian
that are supposed to encapsulate the
behaviour of intersection cohomology complexes when restricted to smaller strata.
This was done in affine type A, in which case the slices are related to moduli
spaces of instantons and Nakajima’s quiver varieties, yielding the
partial results of Braverman-Finkelberg-Nakajima in
[
16
]
,
[
17
]
,
[
73
]
.
It also led, to a definition of these slices in terms of Coulomb branches for symmetrizable
Kac-Moody algebras, see
[
33
]
. Beyond these constructions, the link with the Satake equivalence is still far-reaching.

In this work, we bypass completely this approach and deal directly with the affine grassmannian of a Kac-Moody group
G_{\aff}
, as
infinite as it might be. To approach such a space, the techniques developed in
[
10
]
are extremely useful. Instead of trying to put
some geometric structures on a space like
\Gr_{G_{\aff}}
that do not exist, the key idea is to treat it just as a prestack, focus rather on
the relations between the various functors involved and prove finiteness and representability results on smaller pieces that we can glue
after by general formalism.

This way, we obtain an equivalence of semisimple abelian categories between a category of equivariant perverse sheaves

\Perv_{G_{\aff}\llbracket s\rrbracket}(\Gr_{G_{\aff}})
on the affine grassmannian
\Gr_{G_{\aff}}
and the category
\Ind(\Rep(G_{\aff}^{\vee}))
of
representations of the Langlands dual
G_{\aff}^{\vee}
that we detail below. Note that in this work, because of the amount of technical
difficulties that have to be overcome, we do not investigate the monoïdal structure that is intended to be studied in a future work.
Let us explain our approach in details.

1.2.
Kac-Moody groups

Before diving in the affine Grassmannian of the Kac-Moody (KM) group, several foundational results on KM groups must be settled.
We do not restrict to the untwisted affine case, because the Langlands duality exchanges twisted and untwisted types.
For that purpose, we deal with the general KM case.
In addition we work either over an arbitrary algebraically closed field or over
\mathbb{Z}
,
to pass from finite field to complex numbers.
In this setting few things are known on KM groups.

Associated to a KM root datum
\mathcal{D}
, there is a well-defined
\mathbb{Z}
-form
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{Z}}
of the KM
\mathbb{C}
-algebra

\mathfrak{g}
as well as a completed version
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. The situation for groups is more delicate.
Over
\mathbb{C}
, there are two group ind-schemes:
the minimal one
G_{\mathbb{C}}
, defined by Kumar in
[
56
]
, which is of ind-finite type, and
the formal one
\hat{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
, defined by Mathieu
[
65
]
, which is an ind-pro-scheme.
The formal KM group is defined over an arbitrary ring.
We need the minimal KM group, for which no construction is known over integers.
Over
\mathbb{Z}
, the only available object is an abstract group functor introduced by Tits
[
87
]
, which carries no geometric structure.
This group functor is well behaved on fields or on Euclidean rings only. In the reductive case it does not recover the usual
Chevalley group schemes.
Elementary questions, such as the formal smoothness of
\hat{G}
, the computation of its Lie algebra over
\mathbb{Z}

or its behaviour with respect to base change were not considered.
We summarize Propositions
2.3.1
,
2.5.1
,
2.5.2
and
2.6.1
in the following theorem.

Theorem 1.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a simply connected KM root datum (
2.2.5
) and
R
be a ring.

\mathrm{(a)}

We have

\hat{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}\times_{\Spec(\mathbb{Z})}\Spec(R)\cong\hat{G}_{R}

and
\Lie(\hat{G}_{R})=\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Z}}R
.

\mathrm{(b)}

\hat{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
is formally smooth.

\mathrm{(c)}

There is a group ind-scheme
G_{R}
ind-(affine finitely presented) and ind-normal over
\Spec(R)
such that

\Lie(G_{R})\cong\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R

and an ind-closed embedding
G_{R}\hookrightarrow\hat{G}_{R}
.

\mathrm{(d)}

The minimal group
G
has a closed Borel subgroup
B
that splits as
B=U\rtimes T
, for
T
a maximal torus.
We have
G/B\cong\hat{G}/\hat{B}
, the latter being ind-projective and the morphism
G\rightarrow G/B
is Zariski locally trivial.

We abbreviate
\hat{G}=\hat{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and
G=G_{\mathbb{Z}}
.
We expect that formal smoothness should hold over
\mathbb{Z}
, but we were not able to prove it. Our argument over
\mathbb{Q}

relies on a generalization for group ind-schemes of Cartier’s Theorem
2.1.14
.
In addition to the construction of the minimal group, we provide a construction of the Kashiwara flag scheme
\hat{G}/B^{-}
over
\mathbb{Z}
, as
a formally smooth separated scheme in Theorem
2.3.6
.

1.3.
The geometry of the affine grassmannian

1.3.0.
Cartan and Iwasawa decomposition

Consider now the minimal KM group ind-scheme
G
,
associated with a simply connected KM root datum over
\mathbb{Z}
. Let
T\subset G
be a
maximal torus,
X_{*}(T)
and
X^{*}(T)
the lattices of cocharacters and characters.
Let
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be the set of simple roots.
Consider an element
\rho\in X^{*}(T)
such that
\left\langle\rho,\alpha^{\vee}_{i}\right\rangle=1
for all
i\in I
with

\alpha_{i}^{\vee}
the corresponding coroot, see §
2.2.0
.
We form the quotient stack of the polynomial loops:

\Gr_{G}=G[s^{\pm 1}]/G[s],

where we sheafify for the étale topology.
We switch to Laurent polynomials instead of Laurent series, because

G[s^{\pm 1}]
and
G[s]
are ind-finite type schemes over
\mathbb{Z}
.
On the contrary, the group
G\llbracket s\rrbracket
is a pro-ind-object and
G(\!(s)\!)
is even worse
and we cannot apply the techniques of
[
10
]
to them.
In the reductive case, the Beauville-Laszlo theorem yields an isomorphism of ind-schemes

G[s^{\pm 1}]/G[s]\cong G(\!(s)\!)/G\llbracket s\rrbracket,

and one can check that Cartan and Iwasawa decompositions match on both sides.
So, one can formulate a
Geometric Satake theorem
for reductive groups using Laurent polynomials.
Before going to sheaf theory, we need basic geometric properties, that is to say,
the Cartan and Iwasawa decompositions and the description of the closure of Schubert cells and semi-infinite orbits.
For any field
k
, we have an Iwasawa decomposition, see Proposition
3.2.2
,

G(k[s^{\pm 1}])=B(k[s^{\pm 1}])G(k[s])=B^{-}(k[s^{\pm 1}])G(k[s]).

This decomposition is not new since it already appears in
[
15
]
or
[
39
]
.
However
[
15
]
, although geometric, holds only in the untwisted affine case only, whereas
[
39
]
is only group theoretical
but holds for general types. Here we treat uniformly general types in a geometric way.

In particular, we can define for
\nu\in X_{*}(T)
the quotients

S_{\nu}=s^{\nu}\cdot\Gr_{U},\quad T_{\nu}=s^{\nu}\cdot\Gr_{U^{-}}\subset\Gr_{G}.

Although both sides are not representable, the inclusion
S_{\nu},T_{\nu}\rightarrow\Gr_{G}

are both finitely presented (fp) locally closed immersions.
For
G
symmetrizable, Proposition
3.2.6
yields

(1.3.1)

\overline{S_{\nu}}=\bigsqcup_{\mu\leq\nu}S_{\mu},\quad\overline{T_{\nu}}=\bigsqcup_{\mu\geq\nu}T_{\mu}.

Here the closures have to be understood as the closures of their inverse images in
G[s^{\pm 1}]
, which is an ind-scheme,
quotiented by
G[s]
. The symmetrizability assumption appears because we need some fact about coweights (
3.2.7
)
to reduce to the
SL_{2}

case.

For the Cartan decomposition, we work over a base field
k
.
It is well-known
[
21
, Appendix A]
that the Cartan decomposition does not hold for the full
G[s^{\pm 1}]

but rather for a smaller closed subsemigroup, see Proposition
3.3.2
,

(1.3.2)

G_{c}=\bigsqcup\limits_{\lambda\in X_{*}(T)^{+}}G[s]\cdot s^{\lambda}\cdot G[s]\subset G([s^{\pm 1}]).

For dominant cocharacters
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)^{+}
, we define in Lemma
3.3.3
a fp closed substack

\overline{\Gr}_{\lambda}\subset\Gr_{G}

and a quasi-compact dense open
\Gr_{\lambda}\subset\overline{\Gr}_{\lambda}
.
If
G
is symmetrizable, Proposition
3.3.5

yields a decomposition

(1.3.3)

\overline{\Gr}_{\lambda}=\bigsqcup\limits_{\mu\leq\lambda}\Gr_{\mu}.

We do not have Chevalley’s theorem to prove that orbits are locally closed.
We must prove by hand, in Proposition
3.4.5
, that
\Gr_{\lambda}
is a
G[t]
-orbit in a schematic way and not only on
K
-points, for

K/k
a field extension. In order to do that, we reduce to the formal case, i.e., replacing
G
by
\hat{G}
.
The corresponding
Schubert cell
\widehat{\Gr}_{\lambda}
has better representability properties, although it is by a nasty ind-scheme,
see Proposition
3.4.3
(d). On the
contrary, the functor
\Gr_{\lambda}
is not representable but has a better cohomological behaviour.

1.4.
Affine MV-cycles and the classical Satake

The function counterpart of the geometric Satake for KM groups has already been established, first in the untwisted affine case by
Braverman-Kazhdan in
[
19
]
and then in the general case by
Gaussent-Rousseau in
[
40
]
. The affine
case enables to use geometry, based on the link with moduli spaces of
G_{\fin}
-torsors on surfaces with
G_{\fin}
a
reductive group.
The general case is more combinatorial, using masures, that are partial analogs of Bruhat-Tits buildings for KM groups.
Nevertheless, even when geometry is used, it is only at the level of
k
-points of some moduli spaces, where
k
is a field.
Every
statement there, involves sets, which is why classical Satake is more amenable than the geometric one.

Once the stratifications are introduced, the first test for the geometric Satake equivalence is to study the interaction between
Schubert cells and semi-infinite orbits that deserve to be called affine Mirkovic-Vilonen (MV)-Cycles, or open affine MV cycles.
Here a new feature appears, opposed to the reductive case. In the reductive case, the intersections
\Gr_{\lambda}\cap S_{\nu}
and

\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu}
play a symmetric role and have the same dimension.
In the Kac-Moody case, the intersection
\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu}
is finite dimensional whereas
\Gr_{\lambda}\cap S_{\nu}
is finite
codimensional.
It is important there to take
\Gr_{\lambda}
instead of
\overline{\Gr}_{\lambda}
, where we loose representability.
For that reason, the first intersection was much more studied.
Over a finite field, it was proved that the set
(\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu})(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is finite
in
[
20
, Thm. 1.9]
in the untwisted affine case and in
[
49
, Thm. 5.6]
in the general case,
which is a weak shadow of the finite dimensionality, known as Gindinkin-Karpelevich finiteness.
The other intersection was never considered.

In both works assertions, the finiteness follows from the finiteness of central fibers of the affine Zastavas, that are intersections
S_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu}
.
We follow a similar strategy but we work schematically.
The following key theorem is proved in Proposition
4.2.5
, Theorem
4.3.1
and Lemma
4.4.4
.

Theorem 1.4.1
.

Let
G
be a minimal simply connected KM group over
\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\lambda,\mu,\nu\in X_{*}(T)
be cocharacters.

\mathrm{(a)}

The intersection
(S_{\mu}\cap T_{\nu})_{\mathrm{red}}
is a finite type scheme over
\mathbb{Z}
.

\mathrm{(b)}

Assume that
G
is symmetrizable. The following holds:

(i)

the intersection
(\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu})_{\mathrm{red}}
is a finite type scheme over
\mathbb{Z}
with relative dimension
\left\langle\rho,\lambda-\nu\right\rangle
,

(ii)

the number of top dimension irreducible components of
\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu}
is
\dim L(\lambda)_{\nu}
, where

L(\lambda)_{\nu}
is the weight
\nu
-multiplicity space in the irreducible representation of highest weight
\lambda
of
G^{\vee}
,

(iii)

the intersection
(\Gr_{\lambda}\cap S_{\mu})_{\mathrm{red}}
is equidimensional of codimension

\left\langle\rho,\lambda-\mu\right\rangle
.

The proof of (a) relies on an other interpretation of these intersections as some central fibers of mapping spaces

\Hom(\mathbb{P}^{1},[U^{-}\backslash G/B])
.
We prove a general representability result for these spaces in Proposition
4.1.6
,
and obtain that the connected components of such are smooth in Proposition
4.2.3
, inspired by
[
16
, Prop. 2.25]
.
After that, we need to prove that both definitions of the Zastavas match, which is delicate, because we lack of results such as Beauville-Laszlo
for group ind-schemes or the fact that
(G/U)
is a presheaf quotient, i.e for any ring
R

(G/U)(R)\cong G(R)/U(R),

that holds in the untwisted affine case using results of Česnavičius (see
2.6.1
). To circumvent these difficulties, we use rather a
Beauville-Lazslo gluing assertion for
\hat{G}/U^{-}
, which is a separated scheme (even though not noetherian), to pass from
\mathbb{P}^{1}
to a formal
disc
\Spec(\mathbb{Z}\llbracket s\rrbracket)
for the formal group
\hat{G}
. After that, we need to say that the Zastavas obtained over with Laurent series and the
group
\hat{G}
agree with the ones obtained with Laurent polynomials and the group
G
.

The next two assertions use representability of Zastavas as well as a counting argument. The symmetrizability appears because we need to
use the affine Mac-Donald formula (
[
21
, §7.8]
for the untwisted affine case and
[
2
, Thm. 7.3]
for the symmetrizable) to relate the
Satake transform of
1_{\Gr_{\lambda}}
with the intersections
(\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu})(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and deduce the dimension and number of top
irreducible components by working asymptotically when
q
goes to
\infty
. A similar trick was used by Zhu in
[
91
, Cor. 2.8]
, for the
mixed characteristic version of Geometric Satake.
Note that in order to compare our intersections with the ones of Gaussent-Rousseau,
we must go from
\mathbb{F}_{q}[s^{\pm 1}]
to
\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(s)\!)
(
4.3.4
), identify our maximal compact with theirs (
2.5.3
) and convert
their formula which involves Hecke paths to the affine MV cycles (
4.3.7
).

The last assertion is the most difficult, since we cannot rely on previous results, and even the notion of finite codimensionality is already
hard to make precise. When we pass to the formal group, we have a reasonable notion of
equidimensionality of codimension
\left\langle\rho,\lambda-\nu\right\rangle
but not quite for the minimal group.
We only obtain a weaker
form, which essentially says that after pulling back by some ind-group and divide by an other ind-group, we end up with
something of the right codimension (
4.4.6
).
Also, note that in the formal version, our proof uses the standard argument of cutting by successive effective Cartier divisors.
As opposed to the reductive case where there is a finite number of
\mu^{\prime}s
smaller to
\lambda
, here it is no longer the case.
However, as suggested to us by T. Van den Hove, we can reduce to weights
\mu\leq\lambda
such that

w\lambda\leq\mu\leq\lambda
, for some
w\in W
.
In particular, we essentially sandwich
\Gr_{\lambda}\cap S_{\mu}
between
\Gr_{\lambda}
and
\Gr_{\lambda}\cap S_{w\lambda}

and for the latter, we compute it by hand, because it is a nice and easy intersection.
Indeed, in the reductive case it is just an affine
space
\mathbb{A}^{\left\langle\rho,\lambda+w\lambda\right\rangle}
.

1.5.
The
t
-structure on the affine grassmannian

We now move to the cohomological part. We fix a prime
\ell
different for the characteristic of the base field. It is important to work with
G[t]
-equivariant sheaves on
\Gr_{G}
, because it is
only there that the
t
-structure exists.
The techniques of
[
10
]
allow us to obtain an
\ell
-adic
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}_{G[s]}(\Gr_{G})
that satisfies gluing, i.e.,
we have the four
functors
i^{*},

i^{!},

i_{*},

i_{!}
for any fp-locally closed immersion
i
.
By the Cartan decomposition,
we work on the smaller
category
\mathcal{D}_{G[s]}(\Gr_{G_{c}})
, with
\Gr_{G_{c}}=G_{c}/G[s]
, where
G_{c}
is introduced in (
1.3.2
),
that still satisfies gluing for a ‘reasonable’ stratification by the Cartan orbits (Lem.
5.4.1
).
In particular, to obtain a
t
-structure on
\mathcal{D}_{G[s]}(\Gr_{G_{c}})
it is enough to get one on
\mathcal{D}_{G[s]}(\Gr_{\lambda})
.
Note nevertheless that it will be on the big categories. Now as
\Gr_{\lambda}
is a
G[s]
-orbit, we have

\mathcal{D}_{G[s]}(\Gr_{\lambda})=\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{B}K_{\lambda}),

for
K_{\lambda}\subset G[s]
the stabilizer of
\lambda
. This is a group ind-scheme, thus it is not clear how to define a
t
-structure on

\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{B}K_{\lambda})
. Nevertheless the loop action contracts
K_{\lambda}
to a parabolic
P_{\lambda}
of
G
and, if
G
is affine
(twisted or untwisted), the parabolic further contracts to its Levi factor
L_{\lambda}
, which is reductive.
In particular, in Proposition
5.4.3
we prove that

\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{B}K_{\lambda})\cong\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{B}L_{\lambda}),

and the latter has a well-defined
t
-structure. So far, it is the only place where we need our KM group
G
to be affine,
because otherwise it can happen that the Levi
L_{\lambda}
is still an ind-group and thus
\mathbb{B}L_{\lambda}
does not a priori have a
t
-structure. Nevertheless, some ongoing work of Y. Varshavsky should allow to remove this assumption.
In particular, for any dominant cocharacter
\lambda
, we define an intersection complex

\IC_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{D}_{G[s]}(\Gr_{G_{c}}).

The next step is to construct a fiber functor
F
such that

F(\IC_{\lambda})=L(\lambda),

where
L(\lambda)
is the irreducible representation of highest weight
\lambda
of the dual group.
In order to do that, we must establish an hyperbolic
localization theorem.

1.6.
Hyperbolic localization and Geometric Satake

1.6.0.
Hyperbolic localization

Let
G
be a minimal simply connected Kac-Moody group over an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero or affine in arbitrary characteristics.
We consider the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
\Gr_{G}
given by
2\rho^{\vee}
.
We prove in Proposition
6.3.1
that there is an hyperbolic diagram

\textstyle{\Gr_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{+}}
\textstyle{\Gr_{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{+}}
\scriptstyle{i^{-}}
\textstyle{\Gr_{G}}
\textstyle{\Gr_{B^{-}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{-}}

that is to say that,
\Gr_{T}
identifies with the functor of fixed points,
\Gr_{B}
with the attractor and
\Gr_{B}^{-}
the repulsor. In
addition, the maps
i^{\pm}
are fp closed immersions and
p^{\pm}
are fp locally closed immersion when restricted to each
connected component. In particular, the functors
(p^{\pm})^{*}
,
(p^{\pm})^{!}
and
(i^{\pm})^{*}
,
(i^{\pm})^{!}
are well-defined.
We can thus consider by the constant term functor

\CT_{*}=(i^{+})^{*}(p^{+})^{!}:\mathcal{D}(\Gr_{G})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\Gr_{T}).

It decomposes along the connected components as

\CT_{*}=\bigoplus\limits_{\nu\in X_{*}(T)}\CT_{*,\nu}.

Based on the usual geometric Satake, we prove the following in Theorems
7.1.1
and
7.6.1
.

Theorem 1.6.1
.

\mathrm{(a)}

There is a morphism of functors (
7.1.3
)

(1.6.1)

\CT_{*}\rightarrow\CT_{!}^{-}=(i^{-})^{!}(p^{-})^{*}.

It is an equivalence when restricted to the category of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-monodrodromic objects.

\mathrm{(b)}

If
G
is symmetrizable, then the normalized constant term functor

\CT_{*}[\deg]=\bigoplus\limits_{\nu\in X_{*}(T)}\CT_{*,\nu}[2\left\langle\rho,\nu\right\rangle],

restricted to
\mathcal{D}_{G[t]}(\Gr_{G_{c}})
is
t
-exact.

In order to obtain (a), one cannot rely on the existing versions of Braden’s hyperbolic localization. In the literature, there are essentially
two ways to get it: one is due to Richarz
[
76
]
, which, by a series of devissages, reduces to the case of an affine scheme of finite
type and the other one, due to Drinfeld-Gaitsgory
[
28
]
, that reinterprets Braden’s theorem as a statement on adjoint pairs.

In our situation, there does not seem to exist a way to reduce to known situations, so we must reprove it by hand.
In that
perperspective, the Drinfeld-Gaitsgory’s approach is much more flexible.
First, we prove a contraction principle in Proposition
7.2.1
that allows to reformulate, as them, our problem to a question on adjoint
pairs with the map (
1.6.1
) as the co-unit of the adjunction. The difficult part is then to construct the unit map. This is done
using an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-family
\widetilde{\Gr_{G}}
that interpolates
\Gr_{G}
and
\Gr_{B}\times_{\Gr_{T}}\Gr_{B^{-}}
, with an action of

\mathbb{G}_{m}
.
The key assertion is that there is an fp-locally immersion by Corollary
6.4.3

(1.6.2)

\lambda:\widetilde{\Gr_{G}}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\Gr_{G}\times\Gr_{G},

which is
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariant for the diagonal action on the right hand side given by
2\rho^{\vee}
.
In particular, the pushforward
\lambda_{*}
is defined and by considering the specialization map in Proposition
6.2.1
associated with

\lambda_{*}\omega_{\widetilde{\Gr_{G}}}
, we obtain the desired unit map.
After that, it is essentially formal to get Braden’s theorem and we follow Drinfeld-Gaitsgory’s argument
provided that we have the key geometric statement in Proposition
6.4.5
. Moreover, in order to get the assertion on
(
1.6.2
), similarly to
[
30
]
, we relate this interpolation
\widetilde{\Gr_{G}}
to a natural hyperbolic monoid
{\mathrm{Hyp}}_{G}
,
obtained from the Vinberg monoid, whose construction is generalized for KM groups in §
A
.

Proving the
t
-exactness of the functor
\CT_{*}[\deg]
amounts to compute the image by this functor of
(i_{\lambda})_{*}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}

and
(i_{\lambda})_{*}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}
, for
i_{\lambda}:\Gr_{\lambda}\rightarrow\Gr_{G}
the obvious immersion.
The first image is related to the Borel-Moore homology of the affine MV cycle
\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu}
,
the second one to the cohomology with compact support of
\Gr_{\lambda}\cap S_{\nu}
.
We then use the estimates in Theorem
1.4.1
to obtain
t
-exactness.

1.6.1.
Affine Geometric Satake

We now reach the final step of our work in Proposition
7.7.2
and Theorem
7.7.4
.
Assume that
G
is affine, simply connected, over an algebraically closed field of arbitrary characteristic.
By exchanging roots and coroots in the KM root datum, we consider
G^{\vee}
the minimal group associated to the dual root datum,
defined over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
.
We introduce
\Rep(G^{\vee})
to be the category of integrable
\mathfrak{g}
-modules in the category
\mathcal{O}
, see §
7.7.0
.
It is abelian semisimple by
[
52
, §10]
.

Theorem 1.6.2
.

The category
\Perv_{G[t]}(\Gr_{G_{c}})
is semisimple.
The normalized constant term functor

\CT_{*}[\deg]:\Perv_{G[t]}(\Gr_{G_{c}})\rightarrow\Ind(\Rep(G^{\vee}))

induces an equivalence of abelian semisimple categories that sends
\IC_{\lambda}
to
L(\lambda)
.

To prove the semisimplicity, we consider an exact sequence in
\Perv_{G[t]}(\Gr_{G_{c}})

(1.6.3)

\Delta_{\lambda}\rightarrow\IC_{\lambda}\rightarrow\nabla_{\lambda},

where
\Delta_{\lambda}
is standard and
\nabla_{\lambda}
costandard.
We have

\CT_{*}[\deg](\nabla_{\lambda})=L(\lambda),

as
X_{*}(T)
-graded vector spaces.
We prove that the functor
\CT_{*}
is conservative.
Using the perverse inequalities of the
\IC
-complex, a spectral sequence argument yields

\CT_{*}[\deg](\Delta_{\lambda})\cong\CT_{*}[\deg](\IC_{\lambda})\cong L(\lambda).

Since the normalized constant term functor is conservative, all maps in (
1.6.3
) are isomorphisms.
In particular, when we restrict
\IC_{\lambda}
to
\overline{\Gr}_{\lambda}\setminus\Gr_{\lambda}
, it gives a stronger perversity inequalities
which allows us to prove that

\Hom(\IC_{\lambda},\IC_{\mu}[1])=0,

yielding the semisimplicity.
Since the functor
\CT_{*}[\deg]
is exact, faithful, gives a bijection on simple objects, and the categories on both sides
are semisimple and stable by direct sums, we get the desired equivalence.

1.7.
Remarks on the hypotheses

Throughout this article we work in the simply connected case, essentially for a matter of reference and convenience; removing this
assumption is not a problem, just makes the presentation a lot heavier. As we already explained, the work of Varshavsky should
permit to have a
t
-structure in the general case. Nevertheless, we would still need symmetrizability in order to get the closure
relations (
1.3.3
) and (
1.3.1
) and because the affine Mac-Donald formula is only known in this case. Finally, the characteristic
zero assumption essentially appears because we want to have formal smoothness of
G
that is needed for hyperbolic localization.
Nevertheless, in many statements, at the cost of replacing isomorphisms by topological equivalences, that are enough from
cohomological point of view, it might be possible to discard this assumption. But of course, it would be much more interesting to have
formal smoothness in general. In conclusion, with few improvements we expect to get the equivalence in the symmetrizable case in arbitrary characteristic.
Also, we work with
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
-coefficients, but most of this work should also hold with
\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{n}
-coefficients and should shed light on modular representations of KM group for which little is known.

1.8.
Conventions and notations

1.8.0.
\infty
-categories

All categories in this work are
\infty
-categories, all functors are
\infty
-functors,
and all limits and colimits are the homotopical ones. In particular, ordinary categories are viewed as
\infty
-categories.
A morphism in an
\infty
-category is an isomorphism if it is an isomorphism in the homotopy category.

Our conventions regarding
\infty
-categories follow those of
[
27
, §.0.6]
.
We shall use
[
60
]
as a basic reference.
Let
\StCat
be the
\infty
-category of small
\infty
-categories, stable and linear over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
for a field
L
. In the sequel,
L
is essentially
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
for a prime
\ell
prime to the residual characteristics of the object we consider.
The morphisms are the exact functors, i.e., the functors that preserve finite limits and colimits.
By
[
61
, Thm. 1.1.4.4, Prop. 1.1.4.6]
, the category
\StCat
has all small filtered colimits and all small limits.
The
\infty
-categories we will encounter all belong to
\StCat
.

Most of the
\infty
-categories we will encounter are cocomplete, i.e., contain all small colimits.
A functor between cocomplete
\infty
-category is continuous if it commutes with all small colimits.
Continuous functors are exact.
An
\infty
-category is presentable if it is cocomplete and is generated under colimits by a set of compact objects, i.e.,
objects for which the corresponding corepresentable functor commutes with filtered colimits, see
[
60
, Def. 5.5.0.1]
.
A left adjoint functor is always continuous.
In presentable categories, a right adjoint functor whose left adjoint preserves compact objects
is continuous, see
[
60
, Prop. 5.5.7.2]
.
A corollary of the adjoint functor theorem implies that presentable
\infty
-categories also contain
all small limits, see
[
60
, Cor. 5.5.2.4]
.
Let
\PrCat
be the
\infty
-category of presentable
\infty
-categories which are stable and linear over a field
L
.
The morphisms are the continuous functors.
This category bicomplete by
[
37
, §I.1, Cor. 5.3.4]
.

Given
\mathcal{C}\in\StCat
, we form its category of ind-objects
\Ind(\mathcal{C})
, see
[
60
, Def. 5.3.5.1]
.
It is stable and presentable, see
[
61
, Prop. 1.1.3.6]
.
This yields a functor

\Ind:\StCat\rightarrow\PrCat

which commutes with small filtered colimits, see
[
60
, Prop. 5.3.5.10]
,
[
29
, §1.9.2]
and
[
81
]
.

An
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
is accessible if it is small and idempotent complete, see
[
60
, Cor. 5.4.3.6]
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be an accessible
\infty
-category with finite limits.
Let
{\mathrm{Sp}}
be the
\infty
-category of spaces (=
\infty
-groupoids).
The pro-category
\Pro(\mathcal{C})
is the opposite of the category of accessible functors
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Sp}}

which commute with finite limits, see
[
62
, Def. A.8.1.1]
.
By
[
62
, Rmk. A.8.1.2]
, we have
\Pro(\mathcal{C})=\Ind(\mathcal{C}^{{\mathrm{op}}})^{{\mathrm{op}}}
.
By
[
62
, Rmk. A.8.1.3]
, the Yoneda embedding yields a fully faithful functor
\mathcal{C}\hookrightarrow\Pro(\mathcal{C})
.
If
\mathcal{C}
is stable then
\Pro(\mathcal{C})
is also stable by
[
61
, Rmk. 1.1.1.13, Prop. 1.1.3.6]
.
By
[
10
, Lem. 6.1.2.(a)]
, if
\mathcal{C}
is equipped with a
t
-structure, then
\Pro(\mathcal{C})
is also equipped with a
t
-structure
such that
\Pro(\mathcal{C})^{\leq 0}=\Pro(\mathcal{C}^{\leq 0})
and
\Pro(\mathcal{C})^{\geq 0}=\Pro(\mathcal{C}^{\geq 0})
.
By
[
28
, App. A3]
, for any continuous functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
of cocomplete
\infty
-categories,
we can define its left adjoint as a functor
\mathcal{D}\to\Pro(\mathcal{C})
.

1.8.0.
\infty
-stacks

Let
k
be a commutative ring.
Let
\Aff_{k}
be the category of affine
k
-schemes, equipped with the étale topology.
We abbreviate qc=quasi-compact, qs=quasi-separated,
lfp=locally finitely presented, fp=finitely presented, lft=locally finite type, and ft=finite type.
Let
\AlgSp_{k}^{\mathrm{ft}}
be the category of
k
-algebraic spaces of ft, and

\AlgSp_{k}^{\mathrm{qcqs}}
the category of qcqs
k
-algebraic spaces.
We follow
[
86
, Tag. 01IO]
and call an immersion, a morphism of schemes that can be factored as
j\circ i
with
i

a closed immersion and
j
an open one.

An
\infty
-prestack over
k
is a functor
\Aff^{{\mathrm{op}}}_{k}\to{\mathrm{Sp}}
.
Let
\PrStk_{k}
be the
\infty
-category of
\infty
-prestacks over
k
.
Let
\Stk_{k}
be the
\infty
-category of
\infty
-stacks over
k
, i.e.,
the full subcategory of
\PrStk_{k}
of sheaves for the étale topology on
\Aff_{k}
.
We write

(1.8.1)

\displaystyle\PrStk_{k}=\PrSh(\Aff_{k}),\quad\Stk_{k}=\Sh(\Aff_{k}).

The sheafification functor
\PrStk_{k}\to\Stk_{k}
is the left adjoint to the inclusion

\Stk_{k}\subset\PrStk_{k}
.
All quotients in
\Stk_{k}
are made for the étale topology, unless explicitely stated.

Let (P) be a class of morphisms
f:\mathcal{X}\to Y
from an
\infty
-stack to an affine scheme which is closed under pullbacks.
A morphism
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
of
\infty
-stacks is (P)-representable, if for every
morphism
Y\to\mathcal{Y}
where
Y\in\Aff_{k}
, the pullback
\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}Y\to Y
belongs to (P).
We say that
f
is representable / schematic / affine
if it is (P)-representable, where (P) is the class of all morphisms
\mathcal{X}\to Y
,
where
\mathcal{X}
is an algebraic space / scheme / affine scheme.
We say that
f
is (fp) open / (fp) closed / (fp) locally closed immersion
if
(P)
is the class of (fp) open / (fp) closed / (fp) locally closed immersions of schemes.

For any
\infty
-stack
\mathcal{X}
let
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{red}}
be the corresponding reduced
\infty
-stack.
We say that
\mathcal{X}
is reduced if the counit map
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{red}}\to\mathcal{X}
is invertible.
More precisely, let
\iota:\Aff_{{\mathrm{red}},k}\hookrightarrow\Aff_{k}
be the inclusion of the category of reduced affine schemes over
k
.
The étale topology on
\Aff_{k}
restricts to the étale topology on
\Aff_{{\mathrm{red}},k}
, hence we can consider the
\infty
-category

\Stk_{{\mathrm{red}},k}=\Sh(\Aff_{{\mathrm{red}},k})
.
The restriction functor
\iota^{*}:\Sh(\Aff_{k})\to\Sh(\Aff_{{\mathrm{red}},k})
has a fully faithful left adjoint
i_{!}
.
We define
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{red}}=\iota_{!}\iota^{*}\mathcal{X}
.
See
[
10
, §1.4]
for details.
We say that a morphism of
\infty
-stacks
f
is a topological equivalence if the morphism
f_{\mathrm{red}}
is an equivalence, and that

f
is a topological fp locally closed immersion if
f_{\mathrm{red}}
is an fp locally closed immersion.

1.9.
Acknowledgments

At first, we are extremely grateful to Pierre Baumann and Thibaud Van den Hove. The first, for countably many answers, examples and counter-examples and the second, for this very nice trick that cleared an important obstacle on MV cycles.
We also thank, Alexander Braverman, Kestutis Česnavičius, Michael Finkelberg, Dennis Gaitsgory, Stéphane Gaussent, Syu Kato, Shrawan Kumar, João Lourenço, Olivier Mathieu, Ivan Mirkovic, Dinakar Muthiah, Sam Raskin, Simon Riche, Guy Rousseau, Yakov Varshavsky and Xinwen Zhu for helpful correspondence and conversations.

2.
Foundations on Kac-Moody groups

2.1.
Ind-schemes

For any
k
-algebra
R
we write

K_{R}=R[s,s^{-1}],\quad\mathcal{O}_{R}=R[s],\quad\mathcal{O}_{R}^{-}=R[s^{-1}],\quad\hat{K}_{R}=R(\!(s)\!),\quad\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{R}=R\llbracket s\rrbracket.

We abbreviate

K=K_{k},\quad\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{k},\quad\mathcal{O}^{-}=\mathcal{O}_{k}^{-},\quad\hat{K}=\hat{K}_{k},\quad\hat{\mathcal{O}}=\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{k}.

2.1.0.
Ind-schemes

Definition 2.1.1
.

\mathrm{(a)}

A weak ind-scheme (resp. weak ind-algebraic space) over
k
is a space
\mathcal{X}
over
k

which admits a presentation as a filtered
colimit
X\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}X_{a}
where
X_{a}
are
k
-schemes (resp. algebraic spaces) and transition maps are closed immersions.
If in addition the schemes (resp. the algebraic spaces) are qcqs, we say that
X
is an ind-scheme
(resp. ind-algebraic space).

\mathrm{(b)}

We say that
X
is a reasonable ind-scheme if transition maps are fp closed immersions.

\mathrm{(c)}

If (P) is a class of schemes which is stable by closed immersions, we say that
X
is ind-(P), if each
X_{a}
is (P).
If (P) is a class of morphisms schemes which is stable under base change, then a morphism
f:X\to Y
of ind-schemes
is ind-(P) if there is a presentation
f_{a,b}:X_{a}\to Y_{b}
of
f
by morphisms in (P)
with
X\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}X_{a}
and
Y\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}Y_{b}
.

Remark 2.1.2
.

\mathrm{(a)}

A morphism
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
of prestacks is ind-representable if for any map
\Spec(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}

the fiber product
\mathcal{X}_{R}=\Spec(R)\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X}
is representable by an ind-algebraic space.

\mathrm{(b)}

The colimit is taken in
\PrStk_{k}
and is computed componentwise, i.e.,
X(R)\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}X_{a}(R)
for any

k
-algebra
R
, see
[
60
, Prop. 5.1.2.2]
.
Since a filtered colimit of
n
-truncated groupoids is
n
-truncated,
an ind-scheme takes values into
\Sets
(take
n=0
), see
[
60
, Rmk. 5.5.8.26]
and
[
60
, Ex. 7.3.4.4]
.

\mathrm{(c)}

By Gabber’s theorem
[
86
, Tag. 0APL]
, an ind-algebraic space is a stack for the fpqc topology.
Indeed, each
X_{a}
is a fpqc sheaf and the
finite limits involved in the fpqc descent commute with filtered colimits.

Lemma 2.1.3
.

Let
i:X\rightarrow Y
be an immersion of ind-schemes over
k
.
If
Y
is of ind-ft, then
i
is a quasi-compact immersion.
If further
Y
is ind-affine, then
i
is also quasi-affine.

Proof.

For any ft-closed subscheme
Z\subset Y
,
the morphism
i_{Z}:X\times_{Y}Z\rightarrow Z
is an immersion of finite type, being locally closed in a ft
k
-scheme. Thus
i
is an immersion of finite type, thus quasi-compact.
∎

An
\infty
-stack
\mathcal{X}
is a space if the
\infty
-groupoid
\mathcal{X}(S)
is isomorphic to a set for each
S\in\Aff_{k}
.
Let
H
be a group space acting on an
\infty
-stack
\mathcal{X}
.
A morphism of
\infty
-stacks
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is an
H
-torsor if
f

is an epimorphism in the étale topology, i.e., there are sections locally for the étale topology,
and the action map
H\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}\times_{\mathcal{Y}}\mathcal{X}
is an isomorphism.
We can form the quotient
[\mathcal{X}/H]
.
It is an
\infty
-stack such that,
for each affine scheme
S
, the
\infty
-groupoid
[\mathcal{X}/H](S)
classifies pairs consisting of an étale
H
-torsor
E\to S

and an
H
-equivariant map
\phi:E\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
.
As in the classical case, the quotient map
\mathcal{X}\to[\mathcal{X}/H]
is an
H
-torsor, and if
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
is an
H
-torsor then
the induced map
[\mathcal{X}/H]\to\mathcal{Y}
is an isomorphism.
See
[
10
, §1.2.6]
for more details.

Lemma 2.1.4
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism of ind-schemes which is equivariant for the action of a group ind-scheme
H
,
with
X
ind-separated. Let
[f/H]:[X/H]\rightarrow[Y/H]
be the corresponding morphism of stacks.
Then
[f/H]
is ind-representable. Consider one the following properties
(P)

\mathrm{(a)}

quasi-affine,

\mathrm{(b)}

finitely presented,

\mathrm{(c)}

qc immersion,

\mathrm{(d)}

ind-affine or ind-quasi-affine.

If
f
is (P), so does
[f/H]
.
If in addition
H
is ind-affine, then
[X/H]
has ind-affine diagonal.

Proof.

Given
\phi:\Spec(R)\rightarrow[Y/H]
, we set
\mathcal{X}_{R}=\Spec(R)\times_{[Y/H]}[X/H].

Recall we use the quotient for the étale topology.
Hence, after an étale cover
\Spec(R^{\prime})\rightarrow\Spec(R),

the map
\phi
lifts to
Y
.
Further, the functor
\mathcal{X}_{R^{\prime}}
is representable by an ind-scheme. Moreover the
diagonal
\mathcal{X}_{R}
is representable by a closed immersion, as it is the case after an étale cover, by effectivity of descent of affine
morphisms. Then, it follows from
[
45
, Lem. 3.12]
that the functor
\mathcal{X}_{R}
is representable by an ind-algebraic space.
To conclude, it is thus enough to prove that all these properties are étale local for algebraic spaces and this follows
from
[
86
, Tag 0423, 041V, 0424]
. Finally for (d), it follows again by effectivity of descent for affine/quasi-affine schemes.
Let us now prove the statement on the diagonal.
We have the Cartesian diagram

\textstyle{X\times H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[X/H]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{[X/H]}}
\textstyle{[X/H]\times[X/H]}

with
\Delta^{\prime}(x,h)=(x,h.x)
. But
\Delta^{\prime}
is the composite of the map

\Delta_{X}\times\id_{H}:X\times H\rightarrow X\times X\times H

which is a closed immersion, because
X
is ind-separated, followed by the map

\act:X\times X\times H\to X\times X,\quad(x_{1},x_{2},h)\mapsto(x_{1},h\cdot x_{2})

which is ind-affine, because
H
is.
∎

We will need the following small variant of the above.

Lemma 2.1.5
.

Let
S
be a scheme and
X
be an
S
-scheme with an action of an fppf affine group scheme
H
over
S
.
Then
[X/H]
has a schematic separated diagonal (here we sheafify for fppf topology).
If in addition
X
is quasi-separated, then the diagonal
\Delta_{[X/H]}
is qc.

Proof.

By
[
86
, Tag. 04UI, Tag. 04TB, Tag.06DB]
,
the functor
[X/H]
is an algebraic stack.
Thus, by
[
86
, Tag. 04YQ]
, the morphism
\Delta_{[X/H]}
is schematic and separated.
If
X
is quasi-separated, then in the proof of Lemma
2.1.4
the map

\Delta^{\prime}
is qc, because it is the composition of a qc morphism followed by an affine one,
as
H
is affine thus qc.
∎

Example 2.1.6
.

Let
H
be an ind-affine or ind-quasi-affine group ind-scheme over a base scheme
S
.
Let
T
be an
S
-scheme.
Each étale-locally trivial
H
-torsor over
T
is representable by an ind-affine scheme (resp. ind-quasi-affine) over
T
.
Indeed by Lemma
2.1.4
, the morphism
S\rightarrow\mathbb{B}H
is ind-affine (resp. ind-quasi-affine) and if
E
is an étale-locally trivial
H
-torsor over
T
,
there is a map
T\rightarrow\mathbb{B}H
, such that
E\cong T\times_{\mathbb{B}H}S
and
E
is ind-affine (resp. ind-quasi-affine) over
S
.

2.1.6.
Arcs and loops

We use the polynomial loops functor because it has better finiteness properties.
For any prestack
\mathcal{X}
over a ring
k
, we consider the functors

\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{O}}
,
\mathcal{X}_{K}
on
k
-algebras given by

\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{O}}:R\mapsto\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{O}_{R}),\quad\mathcal{X}_{K}:R\mapsto\mathcal{X}(K_{R}).

Lemma 2.1.7
.

\mathrm{(a)}

If
\mathcal{X}
is formally smooth then
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{O}}
is also formally smooth.

\mathrm{(b)}

Let
X
be an ind-affine scheme over
k
.
Then
X_{\mathcal{O}}
and
X_{K}
are representable by ind-affine schemes.

\mathrm{(c)}

Let
X
be an ind-fp-affine scheme over
k
.
Then
X_{\mathcal{O}}
and
X_{K}
are representable by ind-fp-affine schemes.

Proof.

Part (a) is obvious.
To prove (b), since both loop functors commute with filtered colimits, we may assume that
X
an affine
k
-scheme.
Choose a closed embedding of
X
in
\mathbb{A}^{(I)}=\Spec(k[x_{i}\,;\,i\in I])
. Then
X_{\mathcal{O}}
is closed inside

\mathbb{A}_{\mathcal{O}}^{(I)}=\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{d\geq 0}\Spec\Big(k[x_{n}\,;\,n\in I\times\mathbb{N}]\,/\,(x_{n}\,;\,n\in I\times[d,\infty))\Big).

For
X_{K}
the proof is similar.
Now, let us prove (c).
Both loop functors commute with filtered colimits.
Thus we may assume that
X
is fp and closed in
\mathbb{A}^{N}
for some positive integer
N
.
Since both functors preserve fp closed immersions and finite products,
we are reduced to
X=\mathbb{A}^{1}
.
The claim is now obvious.
∎

An other basic result on loop torsors is the following.

Proposition 2.1.8
.

Let
G
be an ind-affine group ind-scheme over a ring
k
, and
R
be a
k
-algebra. The loop functor yields a
bijection between
G
-torsors on
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}
(resp.
\mathbb{G}_{m,R}
) étale locally trivial over
R
,
and étale locally trivial
G_{\mathcal{O}}
-torsors (resp.
G_{K}
-torsors) on
\Spec(R)
.

Proof.

We treat the case of
G_{\mathcal{O}}
, the case of
G_{K}
is analog.
Let
E
be a
G
-torsor over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}
that is étale locally trivial over
\Spec(R)
.
Then
E_{\mathcal{O}}\rightarrow\Spec(R)
is an étale locally trivial
G_{\mathcal{O}}
-torsor as

E_{\mathcal{O}}\times_{\Spec(R)}E_{\mathcal{O}}\cong(E\times_{\mathbb{A}_{R}^{1}}E)_{\mathcal{O}}\cong E_{\mathcal{O}}\times_{\Spec(R)}G_{\mathcal{O}}.

Consider now the converse.
Let
E
be
G_{\mathcal{O}}
-torsor over
\Spec(R)

that trivializes after an étale cover
\Spec(R^{\prime})\rightarrow\Spec(R).

As
G_{\mathcal{O}}
is ind-affine, by Example
2.1.6
, a
G_{\mathcal{O}}
-torsor over
R
trivializable
over
R^{\prime}
is equivalent to the trivial
G_{\mathcal{O}}
-torsor over
R^{\prime}
with a descent datum.
The descent datum amounts of an element

g\in G_{\mathcal{O}}(R^{\prime}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})=G((R^{\prime}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})[s])

that satisfies a cocycle condition.
Since the map

\mathbb{A}_{R^{\prime}}^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{R}^{1}

is also étale surjective, we can use this descent
datum for the trivial
G
-torsor on
\mathbb{A}_{R^{\prime}}^{1}
.
This yields a
G
-torsor
\mathbb{A}_{R}^{1}
.
∎

Remark 2.1.9
.

For Laurent power series rings
\hat{K}_{R}
instead of
K_{R}
, the situation is more delicate
but the result is still true for
G
reductive with an embedding in
GL_{n}
,
as we were informed by K. Česnavičius.

2.1.9.
Pro-rings and pro-modules

Let
k
be a ring. Let
\Pro(\Mod_{k})
be the category of
k
-pro-modules, which consists of cofiltered projective systems
of
k
-modules. It is an abelian category where short exact sequences are given by cofiltered
systems of short exact sequences of
k
-modules.
For each ring morphism
k\rightarrow R
, there is a base change functor

\Pro(\Mod_{k})\rightarrow\Pro(\Mod_{R}),

M\mapsto M\hat{\otimes}_{k}R.

A pro-ring is a pro-object
k=(k_{a})
in the category of commutative rings.
A pro-module over a ring
k_{a}
is a pro-object in the category of
k_{a}
-modules.
Given a morphism
k_{a}\to k_{b}
the base change of pro-modules is denoted by

M_{a}\to M_{a}\widehat{\otimes}_{k_{a}}k_{b}.

A pro-module over the pro-ring
k
is a compatible system
M=(M_{a})
of pro-modules
M_{a}
over
k_{a}

such that
M_{b}\cong M_{a}\widehat{\otimes}_{k_{a}}k_{b}.

It is countably generated or
\aleph_{0}
if it can be represented by a compatible system
whose index set is countable.
A pro-ideal
I
of
k
is a pro-module over
k
, where each
M_{a}=I_{a}
is an ideal of
k_{a}
.
For any abelian category
\mathcal{C}
, a pro-object
M=(M_{a})
in
\Pro(\mathcal{C})

is Mittag-Leffler (ML) if it is equivalent to a filtered projective system
(M_{a})
with surjective transition maps.
It is strictly ML if it is ML and
\lim_{a\geq b}M_{a}\rightarrow M_{b}
is surjective for each
b
.

An ind-affine scheme
T
over a ring
k
is equivalent to an
k
-pro-algebra that is ML.
There is a functor to topological
k
-modules

F:\Pro(\Mod_{k})\rightarrow\TopMod_{k}

given by
(M_{a})\mapsto\lim M_{a}
and a left adjoint

G:\TopMod_{k}\rightarrow\Pro(\Mod_{k})

that sends
M
to the projective system of its discrete quotients.
In general, the functors
F
and
G
are not equivalences.
It is convenient to specify a class of objects for which this is the case.
If
N\in\TopMod_{k}
, then
G(N)
is strict ML.
Thus, if
M\in\Pro(\Mod_{k})
then

G\circ F(M)\cong M\iff M\ \text{is\ strict\ ML}

By
[
86
, Tag.0597]
, for any countably generated
k
-pro-module
M
, the conditions

M
is ML and
M
is strict ML are equivalent.
In particular, an
\aleph_{0}
-ind-affine ind-scheme
T\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}\Spec(R_{a})
over
\Spec(k)
is the same as a
k
-complete
topological algebra
R=\lim R_{a}
, whose topology is defined by a countable family of open ideals
I_{a}
.

2.1.9.
Algebraic smoothness and formal smoothness

Consider an ind-scheme
X
over a field
k
.
For each
x\in X
we can construct the following ind-affine ind-scheme

\ISpec(\mathcal{O}_{X,x})=\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{x\in Z\subset X}\Spec(\mathcal{O}_{Z,x}),

where
Z
runs over closed subschemes of
X
. It is filtered because
X
is an ind-scheme.
The ind-affine scheme
\ISpec(\mathcal{O}_{X,x})
corresponds to a pro-algebra
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
represented by the pro-system

\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\cong(\mathcal{O}_{X_{a},x})

for an ind-scheme presentation
X\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}X_{a}
.
The pro-local ring
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
has a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{x}=(\mathfrak{m}_{x,X_{a}})
.
Similarly, we can consider the local completion at
x

\ISpec(\mathcal{O}^{\wedge}_{X,x})=\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{x\in Z\subset X}\Spec(\mathcal{O}^{\wedge}_{Z,x}).

Note that we have isomorphims of projective systems

\mathcal{O}^{\wedge}_{X,x}\cong(\mathcal{O}^{\wedge}_{X_{a},x})\cong\lim(\mathcal{O}_{X_{a},x}/\mathfrak{m}^{n}_{X_{a},x})\cong\lim(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}/\mathfrak{m}_{X,x}^{n}).

Further, we have

(2.1.1)

\gr^{p}(\mathcal{O}_{X,x})\cong\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{p}/\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{p+1}\cong\gr^{p}(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}^{\wedge}),

The following terminology was introduced by Shafarevich
[
82
]
and used in
[
56
, Def. 4.3.1]

Definition 2.1.10
.

Let
X
be an ind-scheme over a field
k
.
We say that
X
is algebraically smooth at
x
if

\Sym^{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{x}/\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{2})\cong\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{p}/\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{p+1}
for all
p\geq 0

as
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
-pro-modules.

For a
k
-pro-vector space
V
which is ML, we can form the ind-affine scheme

\mathbb{A}(V^{\vee})=\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{V\twoheadrightarrow V_{a}}\Spec(\Sym(V_{a}))

where
V_{a}
is a vector space, and
V^{\vee}
is the dual ind-vector space of
V
.
This ind-affine scheme is formally smooth. If
V=\mathfrak{m}_{x}/\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{2}
then

(2.1.2)

\gr^{p}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}(V^{\vee}),0})\cong\Sym^{p}(\mathfrak{m}_{x}/\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{2})\cong\gr^{p}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}(V^{\vee}),0}^{\wedge}).

We want to relate algebraic smoothness to formal smoothness.

Lemma 2.1.11
.

Let
X
be an ind-scheme, and
x\in X
.
If
X
is algebraically smooth at
x
, then we have an isomorphism of pro-algebras

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}(T_{X,x}),0}^{\wedge}\cong\mathcal{O}_{X,x}^{\wedge}

and an isomorphism of ind-schemes

\ISpec(\mathcal{O}_{X,x}^{\wedge})\cong\ISpec(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}(T_{X,x}),0}^{\wedge}).

Proof.

By choosing lifts in
\mathfrak{m}_{x}
of a projective system of generators of
\mathfrak{m}_{x}/\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{2}
.
We construct a local morphism of pro-local rings

\phi:\Sym(\mathfrak{m}_{x}/\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X,x}

that yields a map of pro-rings

\hat{\phi}:\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{A}(T_{X,x}),0}^{\wedge}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X,x}^{\wedge}.

By algebraic smoothness and (
2.1.1
), (
2.1.2
), it is an isomorphism on each graded pieces.
Thus it is an isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 2.1.12
.

An ind-ft scheme
T
over a noetherian ring
k

is formally smooth if it satisfies the infinitesimal lifting property for local complete
k
-algebras.

Proof.

By Gabber’s theorem
[
11
, Thm. 2.2.3, 6.2.5]
,
it is enough to check formal smoothness for a ring
R
after a fpqc cover.
Since
T
commutes with filtered colimits, we reduce to
R
a local
k
-algebra of ft and using the completion morphism,
as
k
is noetherian, to
R
local complete.
∎

Proposition 2.1.13
.

Consider an ind-ft-scheme
T
over a field
k
. If
T
is algebraically smooth, then it is formally smooth.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.1.12
, it is enough to check the infinitesimal lifting criterion for
R
a complete local ring.
Let
I\subset R
be a square zero ideal.
We must lift a map
\phi:\Spec(R/I)\rightarrow T
.
Let
x\in X
be the image of the closed point.
Since
R
is local complete, the map
\phi
factors through
\ISpec(\mathcal{O}_{T,x}^{\wedge})
which is formally smooth by Lemma
2.1.11
.
Thus the map
\phi
lifts.
∎

The next statement is a generalisation of Cartier’s theorem. Even the case of an affine group scheme does not appear in the litterature.

Theorem 2.1.14
.

Let
k
be a field of characteristic zero.

\mathrm{(a)}

An affine group scheme over
k
is formally smooth.

\mathrm{(b)}

An ind-affine group
\aleph_{0}
-ind-scheme
G
over
k
is formally smooth.

Proof.

By
[
74
]
, an affine
k
-group scheme
G
is a projective limit of affine algebraic groups
G_{i}
.
In characteristic zero, the groups
G_{i}
are smooth
and the cotangent complex
L_{G_{i}/k}
is in degree zero and projective.
As the cotangent complex commutes with colimits, the complex

L_{G/k}
is concentrated in degree zero, isomorphic to
\Omega^{1}_{G/k}
and flat.
By
[
86
, Tag. 047I]
, the module
\Omega^{1}_{G/k}
is free.
Thus
G
is formally smooth by
[
86
, Tag. 0D0L]
, proving (a).
Now we prove (b). By
[
56
, Thm. 4.3.7]
the group scheme
G
is algebraically smooth. We conclude by Proposition
2.1.13
.
The
\aleph_{0}
assumption is needed to replace pro-modules by topological modules.
∎

Finally, let us quote the following result for a future use.

Proposition 2.1.15
.

Let
k
be a commutative ring.
Let
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
and
g:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}

be ind-fp-schematic morphisms of prestacks.
Assume that
f
has sections étale locally and that
gf
is formally smooth.
Then
g
is formally smooth.

Proof.

Let
R
be a
k
-algebra and
I\subset R
an ideal with
I^{2}=0
. Set
\overline{R}=R/I
.
Let
z\in\mathcal{Z}(R)
such that its reduction
\bar{z}\in\mathcal{Z}(\overline{R})
lifts to
\bar{y}\in\mathcal{Y}(\overline{R})
.
After pulling back by
z
, we can assume that
\mathcal{Z}=\Spec(R)
.
Then
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
are ind-fp-schemes over
\mathcal{Z}
.
By assumption, there is
\overline{R}\rightarrow\overline{R^{\prime}}
étale surjective such that
\bar{y}
lifts as
\bar{x}\in\mathcal{X}(\overline{R^{\prime}})
.
By
[
86
, Tag. 039R]
, there is an étale
R
-algebra
R^{\prime}
such that
R^{\prime}/IR^{\prime}\cong\overline{R^{\prime}}
.
Since the map
gf
is formally smooth, there is a lift
x\in\mathcal{X}(R^{\prime})
of
z|_{R^{\prime}}
, hence
g(x)\in\mathcal{Y}(R^{\prime})
lifts also
z|_{R^{\prime}}
.
Thus the map
g
satisfies the infinitesimal lifting after an étale cover
R\rightarrow R^{\prime}
.
By Gabber’s theorem
[
11
, Thm. 6.2.5]
, for ind-fp-schemes this is equivalent to the usual formal smoothness.
∎

2.1.15.
Tangent bundles

Let
X
be an ind-scheme over a base scheme
S
and
x\in X(S)
.
The tangent space
T_{x}X
at
x
is the sheaf which associates to any
S
-scheme
T

the set of points
y\in X(T[\epsilon])
such that
y=x_{T}\mod\epsilon
.
Since the functor commutes with colimits, by
[
44
, Prop. 5.2]
, we have

(2.1.3)

T_{x}X(T)=\Hom_{\mathcal{O}_{T},c}(x_{T}^{*}\Omega_{X_{T}/T}^{1},\mathcal{O}_{T}):=\operatornamewithlimits{colim}\limits_{Z\subset X}\Hom_{\mathcal{O}_{T}}(x_{T}^{*}\Omega^{1}_{Z_{T}/T},\mathcal{O}_{T}),

where
Z
runs over all closed subschemes of
X
and the subscript ’c’ stands for continuous.
In particular,
T_{x}X(T)
has a structure of
\Gamma(T,\mathcal{O}_{T})
-module.
The next statement generalizes
[
44
, Lem. 5.5]
to ind-schemes.

Proposition 2.1.16
.

Let
R
be a Dedekind ring,
S=\Spec(R)
, and
X\rightarrow S
an
\aleph_{0}
-ind-ft-scheme over
S
.
Let
x\in X(R)
. For all
R
-algebras
R^{\prime}
, the canonical map
T_{x}X(R)\otimes_{R}R^{\prime}\rightarrow T_{x}X(R^{\prime})

is injective. It is bijective if and only if
x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/S}
is torsion free.
Then
x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/S}
is dual to a projective
R
-module.

Proof.

By localizing we may assume that
R
is a DVR. Let
M=x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/S}
.
Write
X\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}X_{a}
.
Then
M
is a ML pro-set isomorphic to the system
(M_{a})
with
M_{a}=x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X_{a}/S}
. Each
M_{a}
splits as
M_{a}^{\free}\oplus M_{a}^{tor}
.
Let
X^{\prime}=X_{R^{\prime}}
and
x^{\prime}\in X(R^{\prime})
be the section given by
x
.
Since
(x^{\prime})^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X^{\prime}/R^{\prime}}=x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/R}\otimes R^{\prime}
, see
[
86
, Tag. 01UV]
, we have

\displaystyle T_{x}X(R^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\operatornamewithlimits{colim}\Hom_{R^{\prime}}(M_{a,R^{\prime}},R^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}(M_{a}^{\free}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime})^{*}\oplus\operatornamewithlimits{colim}\Hom_{R^{\prime}}(M_{a}^{\tor}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime},R^{\prime}).

Since
R
is torsion free,
\Hom_{R}(M_{a}^{\tor},R)=0
and the injectivity follows.
The bijectivity is equivalent to

\operatornamewithlimits{colim}\Hom_{R}(M_{a}^{\tor}\otimes_{R}R^{\prime},R^{\prime})=0

for any
R
-algebra
R^{\prime}
.
Let us show that it implies that
(M_{a}^{\tor})=0
.
Taking
R^{\prime}=R/\mathfrak{m}
, we get that
(M_{a}^{\tor}/\mathfrak{m})=0
and
[
11
, Lem. 5.4.9]
gives the claim. We recall the argument here.
For every
a
, there exists
b\geq a
, such that
f_{ba}:M_{b}\rightarrow M_{a}
is the zero map modulo
\mathfrak{m}
.
By applying Nakayama to
\Ima(f_{ba})
we get
f_{ba}=0
, as wished.
The last claim follows from
[
11
, Prop. 5.2.11]
: by Raynaud-Gruson, a pro-projective module of finite type, which is countably
generated and is a ML pro-set is dual to a projective module.
∎

2.1.16.
Untwisted affine Kac-Moody groups

In this section, we introduce the untwisted affine KM groups.
Recall that for any ring
k
,
a reductive group
G
over
\Spec(k)
is a smooth affine group with geometric fibers that are connected reductive.
Given a split reductive group scheme
\mathbb{G}_{f}
over
k
, we say that a central extension
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}_{f,K}}
of
\mathbb{G}_{f,K}

is of KM type if we have a central extension

1\rightarrow Z\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathbb{G}_{f,K}}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{f,K}\rightarrow 1

that splits over
\mathbb{G}_{f,\mathcal{O}}
, and
Z
is a split
k
-torus.

Definition 2.1.17
.

Let
G
be a minimal KM group over a commutative ring
k
. We say that

G
is untwisted affine type if there is a
k
-split torus
H
and a split reductive group
\mathbb{G}_{f}
over
k
such that

G\cong\widetilde{\mathbb{G}_{f,K}}\rtimes H
, where
\widetilde{\mathbb{G}_{f,K}}
is a central extension of
\mathbb{G}_{f,K}
of KM type.

The main feature of the untwisted affine case is that there is a parabolic
P=Z\times\mathbb{G}_{f,\mathcal{O}}\rtimes H
such that

(2.1.4)

G/P\cong\Gr_{\mathbb{G}_{f}}.

We recall the following theorem obtained in
[
22
]
as a particular case.

Theorem 2.1.18
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a split reductive group over a ring
k
, then
\Gr_{\mathbb{G}}
is a presheaf quotient, i.e., for any ring
R
we have

\Gr_{\mathbb{G}}(R)=\mathbb{G}(R[t,t^{-1}])/\mathbb{G}(R[t]).

Corollary 2.1.19
.

Let
G
be an untwisted affine KM group over a ring
k
.
Let
\mathbb{G}_{f}
be as in Definition
2.1.17
.
Let
\mathbb{B}_{f}\subset\mathbb{G}_{f}
be a Borel subgroup of
\mathbb{G}_{f}
, and
B
the corresponding one in
G
.
Let
R
be a ring such that
\Pic(R)=0
.
We have
(G/B)(R)=G(R)/B(R).

Proof.

Let
x\in(G/B)(R)
.
There is a parabolic
P\subset G
such that
G/P=\Gr_{\mathbb{G}_{f}}
.
Let
\overline{x}
be the image of
x
in

(G/P)(R)
. By Corollary
2.1.19
, we can lift
\overline{x}
to
y\in G(R)
such that

h=y^{-1}x\in(P/B)(R)=(\mathbb{G}_{f}/\mathbb{B}_{f})(R),

where
\mathbb{B}_{f}
is the corresponding Borel of
\mathbb{G}_{f}
.
Since
\Pic(R)=0
, we can lift
h
to
(\mathbb{G}_{f}/U_{f})(R)
for
U_{f}
the unipotent radical,
and then further to
\mathbb{G}_{f}(R)
, which concludes.
∎

We will need the following variant for the opposite parabolic sugroups.
Let
\mathbb{G}
be split reductive over a ring
k
.
We consider the thick flag variety
X_{\mathbb{G}}^{\thick}

given by the étale quotient

X_{\mathbb{G}}^{\thick}=[\mathbb{G}_{\hat{K}}/\mathbb{G}_{\mathcal{O}^{-}}],

see §
2.1.0
. It is representable by a scheme, see, e.g.,
[
90
, Rmk. 2.3.6]
.

Proposition 2.1.20
.

If
\mathbb{G}
is a split reductive group over a ring
k
, then
X_{\mathbb{G}}^{\thick}
is a presheaf quotient.

Proof.

Let
R
be a ring.
Then
X(R)
classifies the pairs
(E,\phi)
of a
\mathbb{G}
-torsor
E
over
\mathbb{P}_{R}^{1}
and a trivialization
\phi

on the formal disc
\Spec(R\llbracket t\rrbracket)
at 0, see, e.g.,
[
90
, Rmk. 2.3.6]
.
We need to prove that
E
is trivial over
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}=\mathbb{P}^{1}_{A}\setminus\{0_{R}\}
.
This follows from
[
22
, Thm. 16]
.
∎

2.2.
The formal Kac-Moody group

In this section, we review the construction of formal Kac-Moody groups à la Mathieu, as group ind-schemes.

2.2.0.
The Kac-Moody root datum

We follow
[
80
]
.
Consider a quadruple
\mathcal{D}=(\Lambda,{\check{\Lambda}},\Delta,{\check{\Delta}})
such that

\mathrm{(a)}

\Lambda
is a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module of ft with
\mathbb{Z}
-dual
{\check{\Lambda}}
,

\mathrm{(b)}

\Delta\subset\Lambda
and
{\check{\Delta}}\subset{\check{\Lambda}}
are finite sets vectors with a bijection
\Delta\to{\check{\Delta}}

such that
\alpha\mapsto{\check{\alpha}}
.

Consider the matrix
A_{\mathcal{D}}=(\langle\beta,{\check{\alpha}}\rangle)
with
({\check{\alpha}},\beta)\in{\check{\Delta}}\times\Delta
.
The quadruple
\mathcal{D}
is called
a Kac-Moody (KM) root datum if
A_{\mathcal{D}}
is a
generalized Cartan matrix (GCM).
The KM root datum
\mathcal{D}
is free if the family of vectors of
\Delta
is free in
\Lambda
.
It is cofree if the family of vectors of
{\check{\Delta}}
is free in
{\check{\Lambda}}
.
Set
Q=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Delta}\mathbb{Z}\alpha
.
There is a morphism of Abelian groups
Q\rightarrow\Lambda
that sends
\alpha
to
\alpha
.
When
\mathcal{D}
is free, we identify
Q
to a submodule of
\Lambda
.
A KM root datum
\mathcal{D}
is cotorsion free if
{\check{\Lambda}}/\mathbb{Z}{\check{\Delta}}
is torsion free.
The elements of
\Delta
and
{\check{\Delta}}
are called simple roots and coroots.
A morphism of KM root datum

\phi:\mathcal{D}=(\Lambda,{\check{\Lambda}},\Delta,{\check{\Delta}})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{1}=(\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{1}^{\vee},\Delta_{1},\Delta_{1}^{\vee})

is the datum of a linear map
\phi^{\vee}:{\check{\Lambda}}\rightarrow\Lambda_{1}^{\vee}
,
and an injection
s:\Delta\rightarrow\Delta_{1}
such that
A_{\mathcal{D}}=A_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}|_{\Delta\times\Delta}
,

\phi^{\vee}({\check{\alpha}})=s(\alpha)^{\vee}
and
s(\alpha)\phi^{\vee}=\alpha
for all
\alpha\in\Delta.

Let
\phi:\Lambda_{1}\rightarrow\Lambda
the dual map.
We say that

\mathrm{(a)}

\mathcal{D}
is a
z
-extension of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
if
\phi^{\vee}
is surjective and
s
is a bijection.

\mathrm{(b)}

\mathcal{D}
is a subroot datum of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
if
\phi^{\vee}
is injective and
\Lambda_{1}^{\vee}/\phi^{\vee}({\check{\Lambda}})
is torsion free.

\mathrm{(c)}

\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a semi-direct extension of
\mathcal{D}
if
\mathcal{D}
is a subroot datum of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
s
is a bijection.

We have the following proposition, see
[
80
, Prop. 1.3]
.

Proposition 2.2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a KM root datum.

\mathrm{(a)}

There exists a free semi-direct extension
\mathcal{D}^{l}
of
\mathcal{D}
.

\mathrm{(b)}

There exists a cofree and cotorsion free
z
-extension
\mathcal{D}^{sc}
of
\mathcal{D}
. If
\mathcal{D}
is free, then so is
\mathcal{D}^{sc}
.
The root datum
\mathcal{D}^{scl}:=(\mathcal{D}^{l})^{sc}
is free, cofree and cotorsion free.

\mathrm{(c)}

If
\mathcal{D}
is free, cofree and cotorsion free, then it is a semidirect extension of a KM root datum

\mathcal{D}^{m}
which is free, cofree, cotorsion free and of dimension
\rk\mathcal{D}+\dim\Ker(A_{\mathcal{D}})
.
∎

2.2.1.
The Kac-Moody Lie algebra

Let
\mathcal{D}=(\Lambda,{\check{\Lambda}},\Delta,{\check{\Delta}})
be a KM root datum.
We consider the torus
T_{\mathcal{D}}=\Spec(\mathbb{Z}[\Lambda])
.
The set of characters of
T_{\mathcal{D}}
identifies with
\Lambda
.
Set
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathcal{D}}={\check{\Lambda}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}
its complexified Lie algebra.
The complex KM algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{D}}
associated with
\mathcal{D}
is the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra generated by
\mathfrak{t}_{\mathcal{D}}

and elements
(e_{\alpha},f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Delta}
submitted to relations
[
52
, §1]
, i.e.,
for
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{t}_{\mathcal{D}}
and
\alpha\neq\beta
we have

(2.2.1)

[h,h^{\prime}]=0,\quad[h,e_{\alpha}]=\alpha(h)e_{\alpha},\quad[h,f_{\alpha}]=-\alpha(h)f_{\alpha},\quad[e_{\alpha},f_{\alpha}]=-{\check{\alpha}},\quad[e_{\alpha},f_{\beta}]=0,

(2.2.2)

({\mathrm{ad}}e_{\alpha})^{1-a_{\alpha,{\check{\beta}}}}(e_{\beta})=({\mathrm{ad}}f_{\alpha})^{1-a_{\alpha,{\check{\beta}}}}(f_{\beta})=0.

We will abbreviate
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{\mathcal{D}}
and
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}_{\mathcal{D}}
.
Let
R\subset Q\setminus\{0\}
be the root system of
\mathfrak{g}

and
R^{\vee}
its coroot system.
We have a
Q
-grading

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{t}\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\in R}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}.

We set
\Delta=\{\alpha_{i}\,;\,i\in I\}
and

Q^{+}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Delta}\mathbb{N}\alpha,\quad R^{+}=R\cap Q^{+},\quad R^{-}=-R^{+}.

Any
\alpha\in R^{+}\cup\{0\}
can be written as
\sum_{i\in I}n_{i}\alpha_{i}
with
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
.
The height of
\alpha
is the integer

\hgt(\alpha)=|\sum_{i\in I}n_{i}|.

The positive and negative Borel subalgebras are

\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathfrak{n},\quad\mathfrak{b}^{-}=\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathfrak{n}^{-},\quad\mathfrak{n}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in R^{+}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha},\quad\mathfrak{n}^{-}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in R^{-}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}.

For any subset
J\subset\Delta
, we define the parabolic subalgebra

(2.2.3)

\mathfrak{p}_{J}=\mathfrak{b}\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\in R^{-}_{J}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha},

for
R_{J}=R\cap(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in J}\mathbb{Z}\alpha)
and
R_{J}^{\pm}=R_{J}\cap R^{\pm}
.
The nilradical and the Levi component of
\mathfrak{p}_{J}
are the subalgebras

(2.2.4)

\mathfrak{n}_{J}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in R^{+}\setminus R^{+}_{J}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha},\quad\mathfrak{l}_{J}=\mathfrak{t}\oplus\bigoplus_{\alpha\in R_{J}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}.

We say that
J
is of ft if
\mathfrak{l}_{J}
is finite dimensional.
Let us consider the cone of dominants characters

\Lambda_{+}=\{\lambda\in\Lambda\,;\,\langle\lambda,{\check{\alpha}}\rangle\geq 0\,,\,\forall\penalty\ \alpha\in R^{+}\}

and the subset of regular dominant characters

\Lambda_{++}=\{\lambda\in\Lambda_{+}\,;\,\langle\lambda,{\check{\alpha}}\rangle>0,\forall\alpha\in{\check{\Delta}}\}.

The cone of dominant cocharacters is
{\check{\Lambda}}_{+}\subset{\check{\Lambda}}.

For
\lambda,\mu\in{\check{\Lambda}}
we write
\mu\leq\lambda
if
\lambda-\mu
is a sum of positive coroots.
The Weyl group is the subgroup
W\subset\Aut_{\mathbb{Z}}(Q)
generated by the simple reflections
s_{\alpha}\in\Aut(Q)
such that

s_{\alpha}(\beta)=\beta-\beta({\check{\alpha}})\alpha
for each
\beta\in Q
.
Finally, let
R_{{\mathrm{re}}}
be the set of real roots.

2.2.1.
The formal Kac-Moody group

Consider a free, cofree and cotorsion free KM root datum
\mathcal{D}
such that

(2.2.5)

\rk\Lambda=\rk\mathcal{D}+\dim\Ker A_{\mathcal{D}}.

We refer to this situation as the simply connected case.
By Proposition
2.2.1
, we can reduce to this setting.
In the rest of the paper, we will work constantly under this assumption, unless explicitely mentioned.
To ease the reading, we may omit the dependence on
\mathcal{D}
, e.g, we abbreviate
T=T_{\mathcal{D}}
.
We have

\Lambda=X^{*}(T),\quad{\check{\Lambda}}=X_{*}(T).

By
[
80
, §2]
, for any commutative ring
k
, there is an integral version
\mathfrak{g}_{k}
of the KM algebra with a triangular decomposition

\mathfrak{g}_{k}=\mathfrak{n}_{k}\oplus\mathfrak{t}_{k}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{k}^{-}
and a root space decomposition.
A subset of positive roots
\Theta\subset R^{+}
is closed if
\alpha+\beta\in\Theta
whenever

\alpha,\beta\in\Theta
and
\alpha+\beta\in R^{+}
.
By
[
80
, §3.1, Prop. 3.2]
, for any closed subset
\Theta\subset R^{+}

there is a
k
-split pro-unipotent group
(\hat{U}_{\Theta})_{k}
with a group and Lie algebra isomorphisms

(2.2.6)

x_{\Theta}:(\hat{\mathfrak{n}}_{\Theta})_{k}\cong(\hat{U}_{\Theta})_{k},\quad\Lie((\hat{U}_{\Theta})_{k})\cong(\hat{\mathfrak{n}}_{\Theta})_{k}=\prod\limits_{\alpha\in\Theta}(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})_{k},

given by an integral version
[\exp]
of the exponential map.
For
\Theta=R^{+}
we abbreviate
\hat{U}_{k}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{n}}_{k}
for the affine group scheme
(\hat{U}_{\Theta})_{k}
and
its Lie algebra
(\hat{\mathfrak{n}}_{\Theta})_{k}
.
In particular, for any real root
\alpha
we have a root group
\hat{U}_{\alpha,k}

with an isomorphism
x_{\alpha}:\mathbb{G}_{a,k}\to(\hat{U}_{\alpha})_{k}
, see, e.g.,
[
56
, §1.3.6]
.
There is an natural action of
T_{k}
on
(\hat{U}_{\Theta})_{k}

coming from the corresponding action on the Lie algebra
(\hat{\mathfrak{n}}_{\Theta})_{k}
.
We set

\hat{B}_{k}=\hat{U}_{k}\rtimes T_{k}.

By
[
80
, §3.5]
there is a minimal parabolic
(\hat{P}_{\alpha})_{k}
with a Levi decomposition

(\hat{P}_{\alpha})_{k}=(\hat{U}_{\alpha})_{k}\rtimes(G_{\alpha})_{k}.

where
(G_{\alpha})_{k}
is the unique split reductive group over
k
with the root datum

\mathcal{D}_{\alpha}=(\Lambda,{\check{\Lambda}},\alpha,{\check{\alpha}})
, see
[
84
, Thm. 10.1.1]
.
The group
(G_{\alpha})_{k}
admits
T_{k}
as a maximal torus and we have

\Lie((G_{\alpha})_{k})=\mathfrak{t}_{k}\oplus(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})_{k}\oplus(\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha})_{k}.

For each
\alpha\in\Delta
, we have an element

n_{\alpha}\in(\hat{P}_{\alpha})_{k}

that normalizes the
k
-torus
T_{k}
and represents the simple reflection
s_{\alpha}\in W
.
All these data are obtained by base change from the case where
k=\mathbb{Z}
.
In the sequel, the constructions may depend on
k
.
Following
[
65
]
, for any
w\in W
and any reduced decomposition
\underline{w}=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{n}}
of
w
we set

E(\underline{w})_{k}=(\hat{P}_{\alpha_{i_{1}}})_{k}\times^{\hat{B}_{k}}\dots\times^{\hat{B}_{k}}(\hat{P}_{\alpha_{i_{n}}})_{k}.

The corresponding Demazure scheme is

D(\underline{w})_{k}=E(\underline{w})_{k}/\hat{B}_{k}.

It is smooth and projective.
The affine scheme

\mathcal{B}_{k}(w)=\Spec(\Gamma(E(\underline{w})_{k},\mathcal{O}_{E(\underline{w})_{k}}))

is independent of the choice of the reduced decomposition
\underline{w}
.
For
w^{\prime}\leq w
we have a closed immersion of affine schemes
\mathcal{B}_{k}(w)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}_{k}(w^{\prime})
,
see
[
66
, p. 130]
.
We define

\hat{G}_{k}=\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{w\in W}\mathcal{B}_{k}(w).

If we complete with respect in the negative direction, we get

\Lie(\hat{U}_{k}^{-})=\hat{\mathfrak{n}}^{-}:=\prod_{\alpha\in R^{-}}(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})_{k}.

We define in a similar way the opposite formal KM group
\hat{G}_{k}^{{\mathrm{op}}}
.

Lemma 2.2.2

(
[
65
]
)
.

\mathrm{(a)}

The functor
\hat{G}_{k}
is an ind-affine group ind-scheme over
\Spec(k)
.

\mathrm{(b)}

The affine group schemes
\hat{B}_{k}
and
\hat{U}_{k}
are closed subgroups of
\hat{G}_{k}
.

∎

Remark 2.2.3
.

\mathrm{(a)}

We will see in Proposition
2.3.1
that the formation of
\hat{G}_{k}
commutes with base change.
and prove in Theorem
2.4.5
that
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}_{k}\cong\Lie(G_{k})
, both are assertions do not seem to appear in the litterature.

\mathrm{(b)}

In
[
56
]
, Kumar constructs a group
\hat{G}^{K}
as an amalgamated product of the normalizer

N(\mathbb{C})
and the minimal
parabolic subgroups
P_{i}(\mathbb{C})
.
By
[
80
, §3.20]
, we have
\hat{G}^{K}=\hat{G}(\mathbb{C})
.

2.3.
Flags on Kac-Moody groups

We keep the assumptions of the previous section and work over a commutative ring
k
. The goal of this section is to prove that the formation of thing flag manifold commutes with base change and give a construction of Kashiwara flag variety over integers, not considered before.

2.3.0.
Representations of Kac-Moody groups

For any
\Lambda
-graded
k
-module
M=\bigoplus_{\nu\in\Lambda}M_{\nu}
with free finite rank weight spaces,
let
M^{\wedge}
be the completion
M^{\wedge}=\prod_{\nu\in\Lambda}M_{\nu}
of
M
,

M^{\vee}
the restricted dual of
M
, i.e.,
the direct sum of the duals of the weight spaces,
and
M^{*}
its completion, i.e., the product of the duals of the weight spaces.
The dual
M^{*}
has an obvious structure of a pro-
k
-module of ft.
In particular, the projective space
\mathbb{P}(M^{*})
is a scheme,
while
\mathbb{P}(M)
and
\mathbb{P}(M^{\vee})
are ind-scheme, see Example
4.1.2
.

For each dominant character
\omega
, let

\rho_{\omega}:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathrm{End}(L(\omega))

be the integrable
highest weight
\mathfrak{g}
-module with highest weight
\omega
.
Let
v_{\omega}
be a generator of the weight subspace of
L(\omega)
of weight
\omega
,
and
v_{\omega}^{\vee}
a generator of the weight subspace of
L(\omega)^{\vee}
of weight
-\omega
.
We abbreviate
v_{i}=v_{\omega_{i}}
and
v_{i}^{\vee}=v_{\omega_{i}}^{\vee}
for each
i\in I
.
Let

U(\mathfrak{g})_{\mathbb{Z}}
,

U(\mathfrak{b})_{\mathbb{Z}}

and

U(\mathfrak{n})_{\mathbb{Z}}
be the Tits integral forms (= the hyperalgebras)
of the enveloping algebras of
\mathfrak{g}
,
\mathfrak{b}
and
\mathfrak{n}
, see, e.g.,
[
80
, §2.1]
.
Following
[
80
, §2.14]
,
for any dominant character
\omega
we set

(2.3.1)

L(\omega)_{\mathbb{Z}}=U(\mathfrak{n}^{-})_{\mathbb{Z}}\cdot v_{\omega},\quad L(\omega)_{k}=L(\omega)_{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}k

We fix
v_{\omega}^{\vee}
to be a generator of the rank one
\mathbb{Z}
-submodule of
L(\omega)^{\vee}
of weight
-\omega
.

Let
V
be a highest weight representation of a KM group
G
.
Then
V
is an ind-scheme of ind-ft, but
\End(V)
is not an ind-scheme in general.
We consider a more reasonable subfunctor.
Following Solis
[
83
, Def. 4.4]
, we use the following definition.
Let
V=\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{a\in\mathbb{N}}V_{a}
an ind-scheme structure on
V
and a corresponding one
G\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{a\in\mathbb{N}}G_{a}
on
G
.
The action map
G\times V\rightarrow V
is a morphism of ind-schemes,
thus for any
a\in\mathbb{N}
, there is an integer
n(a)\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
G_{i}\times V_{i}\rightarrow V
factors through
V_{n(a)}
.
In particular, for any
a
,
b
we have a morphism of schemes

G_{a}\times V_{j}\subset G_{a+b}\times V_{a+b}\rightarrow V_{n(a+b)}.

For any
a\in\mathbb{N}
and any
k
-algebra
R
we set

\displaystyle\End_{a}(V)(R)

\displaystyle=\{\phi\in\End(V)(R)\,;\,\phi(V_{b}(R))\subset V_{n(a+b)}(R)\}.

Then, we define

\displaystyle\End^{\mathrm{ind}}(V)

\displaystyle=\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{a\in\mathbb{N}}\End_{a}(V).

By
[
83
, Lem. 4.5]
the functor

\End_{a}(V)
is representable by a
k
-vector space and
\End^{\mathrm{ind}}(V)
by an ind-vector space.
In particular
\End^{\mathrm{ind}}(V)
is ind-affine.
This ind-scheme depends on the choice of the integers
n(a)
, but it won’t matter for the rest.
Note that
\End^{\mathrm{ind}}(V)
is not necessarily of ind-ft.
There is an obvious morphism of ind-schemes
G\to\End^{\mathrm{ind}}(V)
.
In particular, we write

(2.3.2)

\displaystyle\rho_{\omega}:G\to\End^{\mathrm{ind}}(L(\omega))

2.3.0.
The thin flag manifold of a Kac-Moody group

By
[
65
, p. 45]
, the quotient
\hat{G}_{k}/\hat{B}_{k}
is representable by an
ind-projective scheme over
\Spec(k)
called the thin flag manifold.
Let us review the construction.
By
[
65
, p. 40]
, for any element
w\in W
with reduced composition
w=s_{1}\dots s_{n}
we
have a morphism of ind-fp
k
-ind-schemes

\pi_{w}:D(\underline{w})_{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(L(\omega)_{k}),\quad(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})\mapsto g_{1}\dots g_{n}\cdot L_{w\omega},

where
L_{w\omega}\subset L(\omega)_{k}
is the line of weight
w\omega
.
The source being fp, the map factors through some fp closed scheme.
Let
(S_{w,\omega})_{k}
be the closure of the image.
The locally ringed space
((S_{w,\omega})_{k}\,,\,\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{D(\underline{w})_{k}})
is a scheme by
[
66
, Lem. 141]
.
It is independent of
\omega
by
[
66
, Lem. 141, §XVIII.2]
.
We abbreviate
(S_{w})_{k}=(S_{w,\omega})_{k}
and call it the Schubert cell.
It is a projective
k
-scheme.
For
u\leq w
in
W
, the closed embedding
D(\underline{u})_{k}\hookrightarrow D(\underline{w})_{k}
yields a
closed immersion
(S_{u})_{k}\hookrightarrow\ (S_{w})_{k}
by
[
65
, Lem.1]
.
By
[
65
, p. 45]
, we have an isomorphism of functors

(2.3.3)

\hat{G}_{k}/\hat{B}_{k}\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{w\in W}(S_{w})_{k}.

If the ring
k
is normal and integral, then
(S_{w})_{k}
is also normal and integral by
[
65
, p. 58]
.
The next proposition proves that the constructions commute with base changes.
Recall that a morphism of schemes
X\rightarrow Y
is normal if it is flat with geometrically normal fibers.
We use a more general definition than
[
86
, Tag. 0390]
, that requires that the fibers are locally noetherian schemes.

Proposition 2.3.1
.

Let
k
be a commutative ring.

\mathrm{(a)}

For every
w\in W
, we have an isomorphism

(S_{w}){Z}\times_{\Spec(\mathbb{Z})}\Spec(k)\cong(S_{w})_{k}.

\mathrm{(b)}

The morphism
\hat{G}_{k}/\hat{B}_{k}\rightarrow\Spec(k)
commutes with base change.
It is surjective, ind-projective and ind-normal.

\mathrm{(c)}

We have an isomorphism

\hat{G}_{\mathbb{Z}}\times_{\Spec(\mathbb{Z})}\Spec(k)=\hat{G}_{k}.

The morphism
\hat{G}_{k}\rightarrow\Spec(k)
is ind-normal and surjective.

Proof.

By
[
65
, Lem. 138]
, we have that
R^{q}(\pi_{w})_{*}\mathcal{O}_{D(\underline{w})_{\mathbb{Z}}}=0
for
q>0
.
Hence
(S_{w}){Z}
is flat over
\mathbb{Z}
and commutes with base change.
Since
(S_{w})_{k}
is normal integral,
the part (b) follows from (
2.3.3
).
Since the formation of
\hat{B}_{\mathbb{Z}}
commutes with base change, the part (c)
follows from the corresponding statement for the flag variety.
∎

Remark 2.3.2
.

The ind-scheme structure on
\hat{G}_{\mathbb{C}}/\hat{B}_{\mathbb{C}}
is the same as in
[
56
, Def. 7.1.13, 7.1.19]
,
because
(S_{w}){C}
is normal integral.

2.3.2.
The opposite parabolics of a Kac-Moody group

By Proposition
2.3.1
, we may suppress the subscript
k
or
\mathbb{Z}
and work over
k=\mathbb{Z}
, as we do from now.
For instance, unless specified otherwise we simply write
U(\mathfrak{g})
for the hyperalgebra
U(\mathfrak{g})_{\mathbb{Z}}
.
For any subset
J\subset\Delta
of ft, see (
2.2.4
),
we have the parabolic subgroup
\hat{P}_{J}
of
\hat{G}
associated with the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{p}_{J}
.
We now define the opposite parabolic
P_{J}^{-}
.
Recall that for an ind-affine ind-scheme
X
with a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-stable presentation, one can define as subfunctors the attractor
X^{+}
,
the repeller
X^{-}
, and the fixed point
X^{0}
loci, see §
6.1.0
below.
Since
X
is ind-affine, all of them are representable by closed ind-affine ind-schemes,
see
[
46
, Thm. 2.1]
and
[
76
, Lem. 1.9]
.
Fix a dominant cocharacter
\lambda
of
T
such that
\alpha\lambda=0

if
\alpha\in J
and
\alpha\lambda>0
if
\alpha\in\Delta\setminus J
.
The cocharacter
\lambda
acts by conjugacy on
\hat{G}
.
Let
P_{\lambda}^{-}
be the repeller locus and
\hat{P}_{\lambda}
the attractor one.
They do not depend on the choice of the cocharacter
\lambda
.
We have
\hat{P}_{\lambda}=\hat{P}_{J}
.
We define
P_{J}^{-}=P_{\lambda}^{-}
.
Let
\hat{U}_{\lambda}=\hat{U}_{J}
be the unipotent radical of
\hat{P}_{J}
.
The fixed point locus
L_{\lambda}
is identified with the Levi subgroup
L_{J}
of
\hat{P}_{J}
, see
[
44
, Proof of Lemma 4.1]
.
We have a morphism of group ind-schemes

q^{-}:P_{J}^{-}\rightarrow L_{J}

given by evaluation at
\infty
.
Let
U_{\lambda}^{-}=U_{J}^{-}
be the kernel of
q^{-}
.
We have a decomposition
P_{J}^{-}=L_{J}\ltimes U_{J}^{-}
.
The following is proved in
[
44
, Lem. 4.1]
.

Lemma 2.3.3
.

\mathrm{(a)}

The group
U_{J}^{-}
is representable by a closed ind-affine group ind-scheme over
\mathbb{Z}
of ind-ft.

\mathrm{(b)}

The multiplication gives an open immersion

U_{J}^{-}\times\hat{P}_{J}\rightarrow\hat{G}
.
∎

Note that the authors assume in loc. cit. that the KM group is symmetrizable but this is not used in their argument.
The proof of loc. cit. implies also that for every field
k
,
the group
U_{J}^{-}(k)
is generated by the root groups
U_{-\alpha}(k)
for
\alpha\in R_{J,{\mathrm{re}}}^{+}
.
Hence its base change to
\mathbb{C}
coincides with the opposite ind-group
U_{J,\mathbb{C}}^{-}
considered in
[
56
, Thm. 6.2.8, Def. 7.3.6]
.
Since the map
\hat{G}/\hat{B}\rightarrow\hat{G}/\hat{P}_{J}
is smooth surjective with geometrically connected fibers,
by Proposition
2.3.1
, the partial flag varieties
\hat{G}/\hat{P}_{J}
are surjective and
ind-normal over
\Spec(\mathbb{Z})
. The same statement holds for
U_{J}^{-}
which is identified with an open in
\hat{G}/\hat{P}_{J}
.
If
J=\emptyset
we abbreviate

U^{-}=U_{\emptyset}^{-}.

Taking the opposite formal group
\hat{G}^{{\mathrm{op}}}
, we define similarly a group ind-scheme
U
of ind-ft over
\mathbb{Z}
,
such that for any field
k
, the group
U(k)
is generated by
U_{\alpha}(k)
for all positive real roots
\alpha\in R_{{\mathrm{re}}}^{+}
.

2.3.3.
The Kashiwara flag manifold

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a simply connected root datum.
We now consider the quotient
\mathbb{X}=\hat{G}/B^{-}
, usually called the Kashiwara flag manifold or thick flag manifold.
We also need the partial version
\mathbb{X}_{J}=\hat{G}/P_{J}^{-}
for a ft subset
J\subset\Delta
.

Proposition 2.3.4
.

\mathrm{(a)}

We have an open cover

\hat{G}=\bigcup_{w\in W}w\hat{U}B^{-}=\bigcup_{w\in W}wU^{-}\hat{B}
.

\mathrm{(b)}

The maps
