We want to reinterpret
\Phi
from a smaller diagram.
Following (
6.4.6
) we set

\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{-}=\mathcal{Z}^{-}\times_{\mathcal{Z}}\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}=\tilde{Z}^{-}/\mathbb{G}_{m},\quad\tilde{Z}^{-}=Z^{-}\times_{Z}\tilde{Z}.

By base change, the map
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}
in (
7.4.1
) yields a map

\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{-}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{-}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}.

Composing it with
\mathfrak{q}^{-}\times\id\times\id:\mathcal{Z}^{-}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{0}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
we get the map

(7.5.6)

r:\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{-}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{0}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}.

The map
r
is homotopically ind-schematic,
because it is the composite of
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}
which is fp locally closed and
a base change of
\mathfrak{q}^{-}
by
\mathcal{Z}^{0}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{0}
, which is homotopically ind-schematic by Proposition
5.2.15
.
For each
t\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
, let
r_{t}
be the fiber above
t
.
By Proposition
6.4.4
and (
6.4.3
) we have an isomorphism

\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\cong\mathcal{Z}^{+}\times_{\mathcal{Z}^{0}}\mathcal{Z}^{-}.

Thus, we have

\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{-}=\mathcal{Z}^{-}\times_{\mathcal{Z}}\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}\cong\mathcal{Z}^{-}\times_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathcal{Z}^{+}\times_{\mathcal{Z}^{0}}\mathcal{Z}^{-}.

Thus, the upper term of (
7.5.5
) is isomorphic to
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{-}
.
As in
[
28
, (5.8)]
,
the functor
\Phi
is the pull-push of the diagram

(7.5.7)

\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}^{0}}
\textstyle{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}r_{0}}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}r_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}}

where
p_{1}:\mathcal{Z}^{0}\times\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{0}
and
p_{2}:\mathcal{Z}^{0}\times\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
are the obvious projections.
The morphism
p_{2}r_{0}
is homotopically ind-schematic, because it is the composition of

\mathcal{Z}^{-}\times_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathcal{Z}^{+}\times_{\mathcal{Z}^{0}}\mathcal{Z}^{-}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{+}\times_{\mathcal{Z}^{0}}\mathcal{Z}^{-}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{-}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathfrak{p}^{-}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{Z},

all of which are homotopically ind-schematic, see loc. cit..
Set

(7.5.8)

\mathcal{S}=r_{\bullet}\omega_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{-}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Z}^{0}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}),\quad\mathcal{S}_{0}=(r_{0})_{\bullet}\omega_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}_{0}}^{-}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{Z}^{0}\times\mathcal{Z}).

The same argument as in (
7.4.1
) implies that the complex
\mathcal{S}
is
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-monodromic.
Further, we have

\Phi=\Phi_{\mathcal{S}_{0}}.

Now, we consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}^{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{q}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{p}^{-}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}^{0}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathcal{Z}^{0}\times\mathcal{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}}

The map
\mathfrak{q}^{-}\times\mathfrak{p}^{-}
is homotopically ind-schematic, because
\mathfrak{q}^{-}
is homotopically ind-schematic
and
\mathfrak{p}^{-}
is ind-schematic of ind-ft.
Lemma
5.2.17
for
\mathfrak{p}^{-}
implies that

(7.5.9)

(\mathfrak{p}^{-})_{*}(\mathfrak{q}^{-})^{!}=\Phi_{\cT},\quad\cT=(\mathfrak{q}^{-}\times\mathfrak{p}^{-})_{\bullet}\omega_{\mathcal{Z}^{-}}.

7.5.0.
The natural transformations at the level of kernels

We now want to describe the kernels of the morphisms of functors

\Phi_{\mathcal{S}_{0}}\rightarrow\Phi_{\cT}
and

\Phi_{\cT}\rightarrow\Phi_{\mathcal{S}_{0}}.

We start with the first one.
Recall the closed and open embedding from Lemma
6.3.2

j:Z^{0}\hookrightarrow Z^{+}\times_{Z^{0}}Z^{-}.

By base change and
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-equivariance, we get an open immersion

(7.5.10)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{j}^{-}:\mathcal{Z}^{-}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{-}\times_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathcal{Z}^{+}\times_{\mathcal{Z}^{0}}\mathcal{Z}^{-}\cong\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{-}_{0}.

Moreover, we have

(7.5.11)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}^{-}\times\mathfrak{p}^{-}=r_{0}\mathfrak{j}^{-}:\mathcal{Z}^{-}\rightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{-}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{0}\times\mathcal{Z}.

The morphism
\Phi_{\mathcal{S}_{0}}\rightarrow\Phi_{\cT}
is obtained from (
7.5.2
).
It comes from the morphism of kernels

(7.5.12)

\mathcal{S}_{0}\rightarrow\cT

given by the composition

\mathcal{S}_{0}=(r_{0})_{\bullet}\omega_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{-}_{0}}\rightarrow(r_{0})_{\bullet}(\mathfrak{j}^{-})_{*}(\mathfrak{j}^{-})^{*}\omega_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{-}_{0}}\cong(r_{0})_{\bullet}(\mathfrak{j}^{-})_{\bullet}\omega_{\mathcal{Z}^{-}}\cong(\mathfrak{q}^{-}\times\mathfrak{p}^{-})_{\bullet}\omega_{\mathcal{Z}^{-}}\cong\cT,

where we use the equality
(\mathfrak{j}^{-})_{\bullet}=(\mathfrak{j}^{-})_{*}
and Proposition
5.2.20
.
This morphism of kernels is also obtained by applying the formalism (
5.2.18
) to the diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}^{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{j}^{-}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}^{0}}
\textstyle{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}^{0}\times\mathcal{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Now the isomorphism
\mathcal{Z}\cong\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}
of the fiber at 1 gives an isomorphism

(7.5.13)

\mathcal{Z}^{-}\cong\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{-}_{1}.

Hence the morphism
r_{1}:\mathcal{Z}^{-}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{0}\times\mathcal{Z}
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{q}^{-}\times\mathfrak{p}^{-}
.
In particular, we have an isomorphism

(7.5.14)

\cT\cong\mathcal{S}_{1}

where
\mathcal{S}_{1}=(i_{1})^{!}\mathcal{S}
.
Let us consider now the morphism of functors
\Phi_{\cT}\rightarrow\Phi_{\mathcal{S}_{0}}
.
The specialization map in Lemma
6.2.1
gives a map

(7.5.15)

{\mathrm{Sp}}_{\mathcal{S}}:\mathcal{S}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{0}.

By functoriality of the specialization map, the map
\Phi_{\cT}\rightarrow\Phi_{\mathcal{S}_{0}}
is given by the morphism

(7.5.16)

\cT\cong\mathcal{S}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{0}

equals to the composition of (
7.5.15
) and (
7.5.14
).

Corollary 7.5.1
.

To prove that (
7.5.3
) is an isomorphism, it suffices to show that the composed map

(7.5.17)

\cT\cong\mathcal{S}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{0}\rightarrow\cT

is the identity.

7.5.1.
Restriction to an open substack

Following (
7.5.10
), we consider the open embedding

j^{-}:Z^{-}\hookrightarrow\tilde{Z}^{-}_{0}

and the qc open

\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{Z^{-}}:=\tilde{Z}^{-}\smallsetminus(\tilde{Z}^{-}_{0}\smallsetminus Z^{-}).

Let
\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{\mathcal{Z}^{-}}
be the corresponding substack of
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{-}
.
Taking the fiber at 0 yields an isomorphism

(7.5.18)

\mathcal{Z}^{-}\cong\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{{\mathcal{Z}}^{-}_{0}},

as well as, using (
7.5.13
), an other identification

\mathcal{Z}^{-}\cong\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}^{-}}.

We consider the obvious map

(7.5.19)

\displaystyle\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{r}:\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{{\mathcal{Z}}^{-}}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{0}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}.

It yields the sheaf

\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{\mathcal{S}}=\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{r}_{\bullet}\omega_{\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{-}}.

The open embedding
\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{{\mathcal{Z}}^{-}}\hookrightarrow\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{-}
gives maps

\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{\mathcal{S}},\quad\mathcal{S}_{0}\rightarrow\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{\mathcal{S}}_{0},\quad\mathcal{S}_{1}\rightarrow\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{\mathcal{S}}_{1}.

As in (
7.5.12
)-(
7.5.16
), we have natural transformations

(7.5.20)

\cT\rightarrow\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\rightarrow\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{\mathcal{S}}_{0}\rightarrow\cT

yielding the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\cT\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\id}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cT\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\id}
\textstyle{\cT\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{\mathcal{S}}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cT}

Thus, to prove Corollary
7.5.1
it is enough to show it for (
7.5.20
).

7.5.1.
Proof of Theorem
7.3.1

By Proposition
6.4.5
, there is a qc embedding

Z^{-}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\rightarrow\tilde{Z}^{-}.

By definition
1
1

1

it is an isomorphism over
\mathbb{G}_{m}
and over 0, use (
7.5.18
).
, we have an isomorphism
\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{{Z}^{-}}\cong Z^{-}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
,
hence an isomorphism

\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{{\mathcal{Z}}^{-}}\cong\mathcal{Z}^{-}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}.

Under this isomorphism we have the following identifications:

\mathrm{(a)}

the map
\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{r}
in (
7.5.19
)
identifies with the map

\mathcal{Z}^{-}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{0}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}

given by
\id_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}
and the map
\mathfrak{q}^{-}\times\mathfrak{p}^{-}
in (
7.5.11
),

\mathrm{(b)}

the isomorphism
\mathcal{Z}^{-}\cong\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}^{-}_{1}
in (
7.5.13
) identifies with the identity map

\mathcal{Z}^{-}\rightarrow(\mathcal{Z}^{-}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\{1\},

\mathrm{(c)}

the isomorphism
\mathcal{Z}^{-}\cong\overset{\hbox{\tiny$\circ$}}{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}^{-}}
of (
7.5.18
) identifies with the identity map

\mathcal{Z}^{-}\rightarrow(\mathcal{Z}^{-}\times\mathbb{A}^{1})\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\{0\}.

Thus the composition (
7.5.20
) identifies with the specialization map

\cT\cong(i_{1})^{!}(\mathcal{T}\boxtimes\omega_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})\rightarrow(i_{0})^{!}(\cT\boxtimes\omega_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})\cong\cT

which is the identity according to the proof of Lemma
6.2.1
.

7.6.
The t-exactness of the constant term

We have proved Theorem
7.1.1
using Proposition
6.4.2

which is proved in §
A.2
below.
We now prove that the constant term functor introduced in (
7.1.2
) is
t
-exact.
More precisely, we define the normalized constant term functor

\CT_{*}[\deg]=\bigoplus_{\nu\in{\check{\Lambda}}}\CT_{*,\nu}[2\langle\rho,\nu\rangle]:\mathcal{D}_{G_{\mathcal{O}}}(\Gr_{G_{c}})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\Gr_{T}),

where
\CT_{*,\nu}
is the obvious direct summand of
\CT_{*}
.

Theorem 7.6.1
.

The normalized constant term functor
\CT_{*}[\deg]
is
t
-exact.

Proof.

For this proof it is convenient to equip
G_{K}
with the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action given by the adjoint action of
-2{\check{\rho}}
instead of
2{\check{\rho}}
.
Then, by Proposition
6.3.1
, the attractor and repulsor locus of the induced
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
\Gr_{G}
are
\Gr_{B^{-}}
and
\Gr_{B}

and all maps in the diagram (
7.1.1
) change accordingly.
In particular, the constant term functors in (
7.1.2
) are now given by the following formulas

(7.6.1)

\displaystyle\CT_{*}=(q^{-})_{\bullet}(p^{-})^{!},\quad\CT_{!}=(q^{+})_{!}(p^{+})^{*}

instead of (
7.2.1
).
We first prove that the normalized constant term functor
\CT_{*}[\deg]
is left
t
-exact, using the formulas (
7.6.1
).
By Proposition
5.4.5
, it is enough to prove that for each dominant cocharacter
\lambda
we have

(7.6.2)

\displaystyle\CT_{*}[\deg]((i_{\lambda})_{*}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}[-2\langle\rho,\lambda\rangle])\in\mathstrut^{p}\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}(\Gr_{T}).

Recall the diagram (
7.1.1
).
We also have a Cartesian diagram

\textstyle{\bigsqcup_{\nu\in{\check{\Lambda}}}\Gr_{\lambda}\cap\Gr_{B^{-}}^{\nu}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\imath}_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\Gr_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\Gr_{B^{-}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{-}}
\textstyle{\Gr_{G}}

Fix
\nu\in{\check{\Lambda}}
.
By base change, we get

\displaystyle\CT_{*}(i_{\lambda})_{*}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}=(q^{-})_{\bullet}(p^{-})^{!}(i_{\lambda})_{*}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}.

By Proposition
5.2.20
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\CT_{*}(i_{\lambda})_{*}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}=\bigoplus_{\nu}(q^{-}\tilde{\imath}_{\lambda})_{\bullet}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}\cap\Gr_{B^{-}}^{\nu}}.

Now, by Lemma
3.2.3
we have

(\Gr_{\lambda}\cap\Gr_{B^{-}}^{\nu})_{\mathrm{red}}=(\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu})_{\mathrm{red}}

and by Theorem
4.3.1
the functor
\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu}
is a ft scheme.
Thus there is no difference between
\bullet
-pushforward and
*
-pushforward to
\Gr_{T}

by Lemma
5.2.17
, and we get

(7.6.3)

\CT_{*}(i_{\lambda})_{*}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}=\bigoplus_{\nu}\RGm(\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu},\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu}}).

By Theorem
4.3.1
, the scheme
\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu}
has dimension
\langle\rho,\lambda-\nu\rangle
.
We deduce that

(7.6.4)

\RGm(\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu},\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu}})[-2\langle\rho,\lambda-\nu\rangle]\in\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}(\Spec(k)).

Hence, we have the following relation from which (
7.6.2
) follows

\CT_{*,\nu}[2\langle\rho,\nu\rangle]\big((i_{\lambda})_{*}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}[-2\langle\rho,\lambda\rangle]\big)\in\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}(\Spec(k)).

We are left to prove right
t
-exactness.
By Proposition
5.4.5
, we must prove that

\CT_{*}[\deg]\big((i_{\lambda})_{!}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}[-2\langle\rho,\lambda\rangle]\big)\in\mathstrut^{p}\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}(\Gr_{T})

or, equivalently, that

\CT_{*,\mu}[2\langle\rho,\mu\rangle]\big((i_{\lambda})_{!}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}[-2\langle\rho,\lambda\rangle]\big)\in\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}(\Spec(k)).

By Theorem
7.1.1
, we have
\CT_{*}[\deg]\cong\CT^{-}_{!}[\deg].

We use the right hand side to prove the right
t
-exactness.
We have a Cartesian diagram

\textstyle{\bigsqcup_{\mu\in{\check{\Lambda}}}\Gr_{\lambda}\cap\Gr_{B}^{\mu}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\imath}_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\Gr_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\Gr_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{+}}
\textstyle{\Gr_{G}}

Let
\tilde{p}_{\mu}^{+}
be the obvious map

\tilde{p}_{\mu}^{+}:\Gr_{\lambda}\cap S_{\mu}\rightarrow\Gr_{\lambda}.

Formula (
7.6.1
) and base change yield

\displaystyle\CT_{!}(i_{\lambda})_{!}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=(q^{+})_{!}(p^{+})^{*}(i_{\lambda})_{!}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\mu}\RGm_{c}(\Gr_{\lambda}\cap S_{\mu},(\tilde{p}_{\mu}^{+})^{*}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}})

where we used the isomorphism

(\Gr_{\lambda}\cap\Gr_{B}^{\mu})_{\mathrm{red}}=(\Gr_{\lambda}\cap S_{\mu})_{\mathrm{red}}

which follows from Lemma
3.2.3
.
We must prove that

(7.6.5)

\RGm_{c}(\Gr_{\lambda}\cap S_{\mu},(\tilde{p}_{\mu}^{+})^{*}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}})[-2\langle\rho,\lambda-\mu\rangle]\in\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}(\Spec(k)).

The problem is Zariski local on
\Gr_{\lambda}
and to do that, we consider the commutative diagram (
4.4.6
)
with the maps
i_{0}
,
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
there.
By Lemma
5.2.11
and base change, we have

\displaystyle\RGm_{c}(\Gr_{w\lambda}^{\circ}\cap S_{\mu},(i_{0})^{*}\omega_{\Gr_{w\lambda}^{\circ}})\cong\RGm_{c}(Y_{\mu},(i_{1})^{*}\omega_{Y})\cong\RGm_{c}(\hat{X}_{\mu},(i_{2})^{*}\omega_{\hat{X}})

By Lemma
4.4.4
, we have

\displaystyle\RGm_{c}(\hat{X}_{\mu},(i_{2})^{*}\omega_{\hat{X}})\cong\RGm_{c}(\hat{X}_{\mu},(i_{2})^{*}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})_{\hat{X}}[2\langle\rho,\lambda-w\lambda\rangle])\in\mathcal{D}^{\leqslant-2\langle\rho,\lambda-\mu\rangle}(\Spec(k))

from which the claim follows.
∎

7.7.
Geometric Satake

7.7.0.
The dual category

Let
G
be a minimal KM group over a field of characteristic zero.
The category
\mathcal{O}
consists of the diagonalizable
\mathfrak{g}
-modules
V
with finite dimensional weight
spaces and such that the sets of weight
P(V)
satisfies the following condition

P(V)\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}(\lambda_{i}-\mathbb{N}\Delta),

see
[
56
, §2.1]
. It is an Abelian tensor category.
A diagonalizable
\mathfrak{g}
-module
V
is integrable if
e_{i}
and
f_{i}
act locally nilpotently for all
i\in I
.
Integrable modules are stable by quotients, subobjects and tensor product.
Let
\Rep(G)
be the full subcategory of
\mathfrak{g}
-integrable modules in the category
\mathcal{O}
, which is thus an Abelian tensor category.
The simple objects are
L(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)^{+}
, see, e.g.,
[
56
, Cor. 2.1.3, 2.1.8]
.
Assume that
G
is symmetrizable, then
\Rep(G)
is semisimple, i.e., every
M\in\Rep(G)
is a direct sum of simple objects with finite
multiplicities by
[
52
, §10]
, see also
[
56
, Cor. 2.2.7, 3.2.10]
.
Given a minimal KM group
G
defined over an algebraically closed field
k
, one defines its Langlands dual
G^{\vee}

over
\mathbb{C}
to be the minimal KM group obtained by exchanging roots and coroots in the KM root datum. If
G
is symmetrizable,
then
G^{\vee}
is symmetrizable.

7.7.0.
The equivalence

Proposition 7.7.1
.

Let
A\in\mathcal{D}_{G_{\mathcal{O}}}(\Gr_{G_{c}})
such that
\CT_{*}(A)=0
. Then
A=0
.
So the functor
\CT_{*}
is conservative.

Proof.

Let
\lambda
be the maximal cocharacter such that
(i_{\lambda})^{!}A\neq 0
.
By Theorem
4.3.1
we have
(\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\lambda})_{\mathrm{red}}\cong\Spec(k)
.
Thus
\CT_{*,\lambda}(A)
is just the stalk of
A
at
\lambda
.
It vanishes by assumption, yielding a contradiction.
∎

For convenience, for any
\lambda\in{\check{\Lambda}}_{+}
let
i_{\lambda}
be as in (
3.4.2
) and set

\Delta_{\lambda}=\mathstrut^{p}(i_{\lambda})_{!}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}[-2\langle\rho,\lambda\rangle],\quad\nabla_{\lambda}=\mathstrut^{p}(i_{\lambda})_{*}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}[-2\langle\rho,\lambda\rangle],\quad\IC_{\lambda}=(i_{\lambda})_{!*}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}[-2\langle\rho,\lambda\rangle].

Theorem 7.7.2
.

Assume that
G
is of affine type over an algebraically closed field. Let
\lambda\in{\check{\Lambda}}_{+}
.

\mathrm{(a)}

We have
\CT_{*}[\deg](\nabla_{\lambda})\cong L(\lambda)
as
{\check{\Lambda}}
-graded vector space.

\mathrm{(b)}

The obvious maps

\Delta_{\lambda}\rightarrow\IC_{\lambda}\rightarrow\nabla_{\lambda}

are all isomorphisms. We have

\CT_{*}[\deg](\IC_{\lambda})\cong L(\lambda).

Proof.

For each
\nu\in{\check{\Lambda}}
the functor
\CT_{*,\nu}[2\langle\rho,\nu\rangle]
is
t
-exact.
Thus

\displaystyle\CT_{*,\nu}(\nabla_{\lambda})=H^{2\langle\rho,\nu\rangle}(\CT_{*,\nu}((i_{\lambda})_{*}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}[-2\langle\rho,\lambda\rangle]))=H_{2\langle\rho,\lambda-\nu\rangle}^{{\mathrm{BM}}}(\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu},\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}),

where the last equality follows from (
7.6.3
) and (
7.6.4
).
By Theorem
4.3.1
, we have

H_{2\langle\rho,\lambda-\nu\rangle}^{{\mathrm{BM}}}(\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu},\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})=\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}[{\mathrm{top}}\text{-}\!\Irr(\Gr_{\lambda}\cap T_{\nu})]=L(\lambda)_{\nu}

where
{\mathrm{top}}\text{-}\!\Irr
is the set of top-dimensional irreducible components.
The claim (a) follows.

To prove (b) we first prove that
\IC_{\lambda}\cong\nabla_{\lambda}
.
By Proposition
7.7.1
, it is enough to prove the claim after applying
\CT_{*}[\deg]
.
To do so, we must compute the space
H^{2\langle\rho,\nu\rangle}(T_{\nu},\IC_{\lambda}|^{!}_{T_{\nu}})
.
The characterization of IC-complexes yields

(i)

For
\eta>\lambda
we have
(i_{\eta})^{!}\IC_{\lambda}=0
.

(ii)

For
\eta=\lambda
we have
(i_{\eta})^{!}\IC_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{D}_{G_{\mathcal{O}}}(\Gr_{\lambda})^{\heartsuit}
.

(iii)

For
\eta<\lambda
we have
(i_{\eta})^{!}\IC_{\lambda}\in\mathstrut^{p}\mathcal{D}^{>-2\langle\rho,\lambda-\eta\rangle}_{G_{\mathcal{O}}}(\Gr_{\eta})
.

We want to have the same inequalities with
\Gr_{\eta}
replaced by
\Gr_{\eta}\cap T_{\nu}
.
These inequalities can be checked after taking a smooth cover of
\Gr_{\eta}\cap T_{\nu}
.
We concentrate on the relation (iii),
otherwise the claim is immediate. Using Proposition
5.4.3
(a), we form the Cartesian diagram

(7.7.1)

\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern If we forget the
G_{\mathcal{O}}
-equivariance, the
!
-restriction
(\pi_{\eta})^{!}(i_{\eta})^{!}\IC_{\lambda}

is a direct sum of copies of
\omega_{E_{\eta}}[-d]
with

.
Since
\omega_{X_{T}}\in\mathstrut^{p}\mathcal{D}^{\geq 0}(X_{T})
, see, e.g.,
[
10
, 6.3.5.(a)]
,
restricting further to
X_{T}
yields

\IC_{\lambda}|^{!}_{X_{T}}\in\mathstrut^{p}\mathcal{D}^{>-2\langle\rho,\lambda-\eta\rangle}(X_{T}).

The inequalities (i)-(iii), the dimension estimate in Theorem
4.3.1

and a standard spectral sequence argument similar to
[
3
, Prop. 5.13]

imply that only the stratum
\Gr_{\lambda}
contributes.
Using (a), we deduce

H^{2\langle\rho,\nu\rangle}(T_{\nu},\IC_{\lambda}|^{!}_{T_{\nu}})=H^{2\langle\rho,\nu\rangle}(Y_{T},\IC_{\lambda}|_{\Gr_{\lambda}})=H_{2\langle\rho,\lambda-\nu\rangle}^{\mathrm{BM}}(Y_{T},\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})=L(\lambda)_{\nu}.

In particular, we have

\CT_{*}(\IC_{\lambda})=\CT_{*}(\nabla_{\lambda}).

By Proposition
7.7.1
, this yields an isomorphism
\IC_{\lambda}\cong\nabla_{\lambda}
.

Next, we prove that
\Delta_{\lambda}\cong\IC_{\lambda}
.
We must compute
H_{c}^{2\langle\rho,\mu\rangle}(S_{\mu},\IC_{\lambda})
.
The strategy is the same.
We must prove that only the stratum
\Gr_{\lambda}
contributes.
In that case, using the
t
-exactness and the isomorphisms

\Delta_{\lambda}|_{\Gr_{\lambda}}\cong\IC_{\lambda}|_{\Gr_{\lambda}}\cong\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}}[-2\langle\rho,\lambda\rangle],

we get

\CT_{*,\mu}(\IC_{\lambda})=H^{2\langle\rho,\mu\rangle}_{c}(S_{\mu}\cap\Gr_{\lambda},\IC_{\lambda})=H_{c}^{2\langle\rho,\mu\rangle}(S_{\mu},\Delta_{\lambda})=H_{c}^{-2\langle\rho,\lambda-\mu\rangle}(S_{\mu},s_{\mu}^{*}\omega_{\Gr_{\lambda}})=\CT_{*,\mu}(\Delta_{\lambda}).

It is sufficient to prove that for all
\eta<\lambda

(7.7.2)

\RGm_{c}(S_{\mu}\cap\Gr_{\eta},\IC_{\lambda})\in\mathcal{D}^{<-2\langle\rho,\lambda-\mu\rangle}(\Spec(k)).

First, by definition of the IC complex (
5.3.3
) we have

(i_{\eta})^{*}\IC_{\lambda}\in\mathstrut^{p}\mathcal{D}^{<-2\langle\rho,\lambda-\eta\rangle}_{G_{\mathcal{O}}}(\Gr_{\eta}).

The map
\pi_{\eta}
is smooth and surjective.
Thus the functor
(\pi_{\eta})^{!}
is
t
-exact.
Thus, we have

(\pi_{\eta})^{!}(i_{\eta})^{*}\IC_{\lambda}\in\mathstrut^{p}\mathcal{D}^{<-2\langle\rho,\lambda-\eta\rangle}_{G_{\mathcal{O}}}(E_{\eta})

Further, the sheaf
(i_{\eta})^{*}\IC_{\lambda}
being
G(\mathcal{O})
-equivariant,
it can be written as
h^{!}K_{0}
for some
K_{0}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{B}L_{\eta})
.
By the commutativity of (
7.7.1
),
the sheaf
(\pi_{\eta})^{!}(i_{\eta})^{*}\IC_{\lambda}
is a direct sum of
\omega_{E_{\eta}}[-d]
for
d<-2\langle\rho,\lambda-\eta\rangle
.
We form the Cartesian square

\textstyle{X_{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{E_{\eta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\eta}}
\textstyle{Y_{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{\mu}}
\textstyle{\Gr_{\eta}}

Using smooth descent and Lurie’s adjunction, for a sheaf
K\in\mathcal{D}(Y_{S})
, we have an equivalence

K\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{[n]}(\pi^{[n]})_{!}(\pi^{[n]})^{!}K,

where the map

\pi^{[n]}:X_{S}^{[n]}\rightarrow Y_{S}

is as in (
5.1.5
).
This gives an isomorphism

\RGm_{c}(Y_{S},K)\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{[n]}\RGm_{c}(X_{S}^{[n]},(\pi^{[n]})^{!}K).

Since the category
\mathcal{D}^{<-2\langle\rho,\lambda-\eta\rangle}(\Spec(k))
is stable by colimits,
to prove (
7.7.2
) it is enough to prove it on the various
X_{S}^{[n]}
for the sheaf

K=\IC_{\lambda}|_{Y_{S}}=(s_{\mu})^{*}(i_{\eta})^{*}\IC_{\lambda}.

By smoothness, we have

(\pi^{[n]})^{!}K\cong(s^{[n]})^{*}((\pi_{\eta})^{[n]})^{!}(i_{\eta})^{*}\IC_{\lambda}

The complex
(\pi^{[n]})^{!}K
is a direct sum of complexes

(s^{[n]})^{*}\omega_{(E_{\eta})^{[n]}}[-d]=(\pi^{[n]})^{!}(s_{\mu})^{*}\omega_{\Gr_{\eta}}[-d]

with
d<-2\langle\rho,\lambda-\eta\rangle
.
Thus the claim follows from (
7.6.5
) and the following lemma.

Lemma 7.7.3
.

If
K\in\mathcal{D}(Y_{S})
is such that
\RGm_{c}(Y,K)\in\mathcal{D}^{<0}(\Spec(k))
, then

\RGm_{c}(X_{S}^{[n]},(\pi^{[n]})^{!}K)\in\mathcal{D}^{<0}(\Spec(k)).

Proof.

Considering the tower

X_{S}^{[n]}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow X_{S}\rightarrow Y_{S}.

By induction on
n
, we can assume that
n=1
.
Since the map
\pi
is smooth, the projection formula gives

\pi_{!}\pi^{!}K\cong K\overset{!}{\otimes}\pi_{!}\omega_{X_{S}}\cong K\overset{!}{\otimes}{f}^{!}\bar{\pi}_{!}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}

where the maps
\overline{\pi}:\Spec(k)\rightarrow\mathbb{B}L_{\lambda}
and
f:Y\rightarrow\mathbb{B}L_{\lambda}
are the obvious ones.
Here the last base change follows from (
5.2.11
).
Since
L_{\lambda}
is geometrically connected, the sheaf
\mathstrut{}^{p}\mathcal{H}^{i}(\bar{\pi}_{!}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})
is constant.
Further, we have
\bar{\pi}_{!}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}\in\mathstrut^{p}\mathcal{D}^{\leq 0}(\mathbb{B}L_{\lambda})
.
Filtering the complex
\overline{\pi}_{!}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
, an induction using the smoothness of
\mathbb{B}L_{\lambda}
implies that we can replace

\overline{\pi}_{!}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
by the dualizing sheaf
\omega_{\mathbb{B}L_{\lambda}}
.
Thus, the claim reduces to prove that

\RGm_{c}(Y_{S},K)\in\mathcal{D}^{<0}(\Spec(k))
which holds by assumption.
∎

∎

We have the normalized constant term functor:

\CT_{*}[\deg]:\mathcal{D}_{G_{\mathcal{O}}}(\Gr_{G_{c}})^{\heartsuit}\Ind(\Rep(T^{\vee})),

as well as a canonical restriction functor:

\Rep(G^{\vee})\rightarrow\Rep(T^{\vee}),

that is faithful and injective on objects. In particular, it identifies
\Rep(G^{\vee})
as a non-full subcategory of
\Rep(T^{\vee})
.

Theorem 7.7.4
.

The category
\mathcal{D}_{G_{\mathcal{O}}}(\Gr_{G_{c}})^{\heartsuit}
is semisimple.
The normalized constant term
\CT_{*}[\deg]
is an equivalence of semisimple Abelian categories

\mathcal{D}_{G_{\mathcal{O}}}(\Gr_{G_{c}})^{\heartsuit}\cong\Ind(\Rep(G^{\vee})).

Proof.

By Theorem
7.6.1
and Proposition
7.7.1
,
the functor is exact and conservative, thus faithful.
By Corollary
5.4.6
, the simple objects are the same and
\Rep(G^{\vee})
is semisimple.
Let us prove that
\mathcal{D}_{G_{\mathcal{O}}}(\Gr_{G_{c}})^{\heartsuit}
is semisimple.
We must prove that for any
\lambda,\mu\in X_{*}(T)
, we have

\Hom_{\mathcal{D}_{G_{\mathcal{O}}}(\Gr_{G_{c}})}(\IC_{\lambda},\IC_{\mu}[1])=0.

In the reductive case, the usual argument uses parity vanishing of
\mathcal{H}^{i}(\IC_{\lambda})
.
In our situation, we do not know how to prove
such a parity result. Nevertheless, to prove semisimplicity we need less.
The argument of
[
77
, Prop. 3.1]
gives that we only need for
\mu<\lambda
and
\mu
dominant that

(7.7.3)

(i_{\mu})^{!}\IC_{\lambda}\in\mathstrut^{p}\mathcal{D}^{\geq-2\langle\rho,\lambda-\mu\rangle+2}(\Gr_{\mu}),\quad(i_{\mu})^{*}\IC_{\lambda}\in\mathstrut^{p}\mathcal{D}^{\leq-2\langle\rho,\lambda-\mu\rangle-2}(\Gr_{\mu}).

The usual inequality for an IC complex is (
5.3.3
) which yields

(i_{\mu})^{!}\IC_{\lambda}\in\mathstrut^{p}\mathcal{D}^{\geq-2\langle\rho,\lambda-\mu\rangle+1}(\Gr_{\mu}),\quad(i_{\mu})^{*}\IC_{\lambda}\in\mathstrut^{p}\mathcal{D}^{\leq-2\langle\rho,\lambda-\mu\rangle-1}(\Gr_{\mu}).

However, the stronger inequality follows from Theorem
7.7.2
(b) and
[
4
, Rmk. after Cor. 1.4.24]
.
Thus, the category
\mathcal{D}_{G_{\mathcal{O}}}(\Gr_{G_{c}})^{\heartsuit}
is semisimple and the functor is fully faithful.
As both sides are stable by arbitrary direct sums and
\Rep(G^{\vee})
is already in the image of the functor, we obtain the desired
equivalence.
∎

Appendix A
The Vinberg monoid of a Kac-Moody group

The goal of this section is to give a proof of Proposition
6.4.2
.
To do this, we first gather some material on Vinberg monoids of KM groups.
The main result of this section is Proposition
A.2.2
.
We will assume that
k
is an algebraically closed field, and
G
is a simply connected minimal KM group over
k
.
We also assume that either
G
is arbitrary and
\car(k)=0
,
or
G
is affine and
\car(k)
is arbitrary.

A.1.
Construction

We consider the group ind-scheme

G_{+}=(G\times T)/Z

where we embed the center
Z
of
G
anti-diagonally.
Let

T_{+}=(T\times T)/Z,\quad Z_{+}=(Z\times T)/Z\cong T

be the maximal torus and the center of
G_{+}
.
For each dominant character
\omega
, let

\rho_{\omega}
be as in (
2.3.2
).
Set

H_{G}=\prod\limits_{i\in I}\End^{\mathrm{ind}}(L(\omega_{i}))\times\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}

where
\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}
is as in (
4.2.3
).
The map

G_{+}\to H_{G}
given by

(g,t)\mapsto(\omega_{i}(t)\rho_{\omega_{i}}(g),\alpha(t))

is a monomorphism.
We define
\Vin_{G}
to be the scheme theoretic image of
G_{+}
in
H_{G}
.
The scheme theoretic image commutes with filtered colimits.
The scheme theoretic image of a morphism of schemes
f:X\to Y
is the smallest closed subscheme
Z\subset Y

through which
f
factors, see
[
86
, Tag. 01R7]
.
Thus the functor
\Vin_{G}
is the colimit of the closures of the images of the components of a colimit representing
G
.
The functor
\Vin_{G}
has an action of
G_{+}\times G_{+}
that extends the left and right multiplication on
G_{+}
.
We deduce the following.

Proposition A.1.1
.

\Vin_{G}
is a
G_{+}\times G_{+}
-equivariant ind-affine ind-scheme with a monoid structure.
∎

We call
\Vin_{G}
the Vinberg monoid of
G
.
Since
\End^{\mathrm{ind}}(V)
may not be of ind-finite type, the Vinberg monoid may also not be of ind-finite type,
see §
2.3.0
.
We identify
G_{+}
with its image in
\Vin_{G}
.
Let
T_{\mathrm{ad}}=T/Z
.
The group
T_{\mathrm{ad}}
embeds in
\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}
via the simple roots as the open subset where all coordinates are nonzero.
Let
T_{\Delta}\subset\Vin_{G}
be the image of the anti-diagonal morphism
T\to T_{+}
.
Let
\overline{T}_{\Delta}\subset\Vin_{G}
be its closure.
The projection
H_{G}\to\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}
gives a map

\det:\Vin_{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}.

This map restricts to an isomorphism

(A.1.1)

T_{\Delta}\cong T_{\mathrm{ad}}.

For each
t\in T_{\Delta}
and each
i\in I
, the endomorphism
\omega_{i}(t^{-1})\rho_{\omega_{i}}(t)
is polynomial in the simple roots
\alpha(t)
.
Hence the isomorphism (
A.1.1
) extends to an isomorphism

(A.1.2)

\overline{T}_{\Delta}\cong\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}

This yields the section

(A.1.3)

\sigma=(\det)^{-1}:\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}\rightarrow\overline{T}_{\Delta}\subset\Vin_{G}.

We introduce the open Bruhat cell in
\Vin_{G}
following the strategy of Solis in
[
83
]
.
For a vector space
V
and an element
v^{\vee}\in V^{\vee}
, we consider the qc open subsets in
V
and
\mathbb{P}(V)
given by

V_{v}=V\setminus\{v^{\vee}=0\},\quad\mathbb{P}(V)_{v}=\mathbb{P}(V)\smallsetminus\mathbb{P}(\{v^{\vee}=0\}).

We have an
U^{-}\times U
-equivariant map

\psi:H\rightarrow\prod_{i\in I}L(\omega_{i})\times L(\omega_{i})^{\vee},\quad(f,z)\mapsto(f(v_{i}),v_{i}^{\vee}f)_{i\in I}

We consider the qc open subset

H_{\Omega}\subset H_{G}

given by

H_{\Omega}=\psi^{-1}\Big(\prod_{i\in I}L(\omega_{i})_{v_{i}}\times L(\omega_{i})_{v_{i}^{\vee}}^{\vee}\Big).

As
\Vin_{G}
is closed in
H_{G}
, the qc open subset
\Vin_{\Omega}\subset\Vin_{G}
given by

\Vin_{\Omega}=\Vin_{G}\cap H_{\Omega}

is closed in
H_{\Omega}
.
Let
\Omega_{+}=U^{-}T_{+}U
be the open cell of
G_{+}
.
We have

\Omega_{+}=G_{+}\cap\Vin_{\Omega},\quad\overline{T}_{\Delta}\subset\Vin_{\Omega}.

Quotienting by
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, we obtain a map

\overline{\psi}:H_{\Omega}\rightarrow\prod_{i\in I}\mathbb{P}(L(\omega_{i}))_{v_{i}}\times\mathbb{P}(L(\omega_{i})^{\vee})_{v_{i}^{\vee}}.

Further, the orbit maps at the tuples
(v_{i},v_{i}^{\vee})
yield the map

b:G_{+}\rightarrow\prod_{i\in I}\mathbb{P}(L(\omega_{i}))\times\mathbb{P}(L(\omega_{i})^{\vee})

The map
b
coincides with
\overline{\psi}
over the open cell
\Omega_{+}
.
By Lemma
2.3.5
, the stabilizer of the tuple
(v_{i},v_{i}^{\vee})
in
G_{+}\times G_{+}
identifies with
B_{+}\times B_{+}^{-}
.
Hence the map
b
factors through

G_{+}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta}}{{\rightarrow}}G_{+}\times G_{+}\rightarrow G_{+}/B_{+}\times G_{+}/B_{+}^{-}=G/B\times G/B^{-}.

Over
\Omega_{+}
the maps
b
and
\overline{\psi}
are both given by the projection followed by the open immersion

(A.1.4)

U^{-}\times T_{+}\times U\rightarrow U^{-}\times U\hookrightarrow G/B\times G/B^{-}.

Lemma A.1.2
.

\mathrm{(a)}

U^{-}\times U
is closed in
\prod_{i\in I}\mathbb{P}(L(\omega_{i}))_{v_{i}}\times\mathbb{P}(L(\omega_{i})^{\vee})_{v_{i}^{\vee}}
.

\mathrm{(b)}

\overline{\psi}(\Vin_{\Omega})=U^{-}\times U
.

Proof.

Part (a) follows from (
2.4.5
).
To prove (b), note that
\Omega_{+}
is dense in
G_{+}
.
Hence
\Omega_{+}
is dense in
\Vin_{\Omega}
.
By (
A.1.4
), we have
\overline{\psi}(\Omega_{+})=U^{-}\times U
, which is closed in the product

\prod_{i\in I}\mathbb{P}(L(\omega_{i}))_{v_{i}}\times\mathbb{P}(L(\omega_{i})^{\vee})_{v_{i}^{\vee}}.

Part (b) follows.
∎

Proposition A.1.3
.

\mathrm{(a)}

The map

U^{-}\times Z_{+}\times\overline{T}_{\Delta}\times U\rightarrow\Vin_{\Omega}

given by
(u^{-},z,t,u)\mapsto u^{-}ztu
is an isomorphism.

\mathrm{(b)}

\Omega_{+}
is a qc open of
\Vin_{\Omega}
.

\mathrm{(c)}

\Vin_{\Omega}
is an ind-ft-scheme.

Proof.

(c) follows from (a).
Let
\eta:U^{-}\times Z_{+}\times\overline{T}_{\Delta}\times U\rightarrow\Vin_{\Omega}
be the map in (a).
By Lemma
A.1.2
, we have

\overline{\psi}(\Vin_{\Omega})\subset U^{-}\times U
.
Since the map
\overline{\psi}
is
U^{-}\times U
-equivariant,
to prove (a) it is enough to check that

Z_{+}\times\overline{T}_{\Delta}=\overline{\psi}^{-1}(1,1).

The inclusion
\subset
is immediate, because
Z_{+}
acts by dilatation on each
L(\omega_{i})
.
Let us prove the converse.
Set

H_{0}=\Big(\prod_{i\in I}\big(\End^{\mathrm{ind}}(L(\omega_{i}))\setminus\{0\}\big)\Big).

The group
Z_{+}
acts freely on
H_{0}
, because it acts by dilatation on each factor.
The functor
H_{0}/Z_{+}
is representable by

\prod_{i\in I}\mathbb{P}\Big(\End^{\mathrm{ind}}(L(\omega_{i}))\Big).

We have
\Vin_{\Omega}\subset H_{\Omega}\subset H_{0}
.
Since
\Vin_{\Omega}
is closed in
H_{\Omega}
, it is locally closed in
H_{0}
.
Hence, the functor
\Vin_{\Omega}/Z_{+}
is also representable by an ind-scheme.
We have
T_{+}\cong Z_{+}\times T_{\Delta}.
and as
\overline{T}_{\Delta}
is closed in
\Vin_{\Omega}/Z_{+}
,
Z_{+}\times\overline{T}_{\Delta}
is closed in
\Vin_{\Omega}
.
Hence
Z_{+}\times\overline{T}_{\Delta}
is the closure of
T_{+}
in
\Vin_{\Omega}
.
Let
x
be a
k
-point in
\overline{\psi}^{-1}(1,1)
.
By
[
86
, Tag. 02JQ]
, there is a valuation ring
R
with fraction field
K
and
a function
f\in(U^{-}T_{+}U)(K)
that extends to a function
\tilde{f}\in\Vin_{\Omega}(R)
,
and such that
f(\mathfrak{m})=x
where
\mathfrak{m}
is the closed point of
k
. Let

(u^{-},u)=\overline{\psi}(\tilde{f})
in
(U^{-}\times U)(R)
.
We have

((u^{-},u)^{-1}\tilde{f})(\mathfrak{m})=x,\quad((u^{-},u)^{-1}\tilde{f})|_{\Spec(K)}\in T_{+}(K).

Thus the map
x
is in the closure of
T_{+}
which is
Z_{+}\times\overline{T}_{\Delta}
.
We deduce that the map
\eta
is a closed immersion which is bijective on
k
-points.
Thus it is an homeomorphism.
We deduce that
\Omega_{+}
is open in
\Vin_{\Omega}
and thus in
\Vin_{G}
and as
\Omega_{+}
is ind-affine, we get (b).

Since
\Omega_{+}
is schematically dense in
G_{+}
and quasi-compact by (b),
the ind-scheme
\Vin_{G}
is also the scheme theoretic image of
\Omega_{+}
.
Thus
[
86
, Tag. 01R8, 01RD]
implies that
\Omega_{+}
is schematically dense in
\Vin_{\Omega}
, i.e.,
for any open subset
U\subset\Vin_{\Omega}
the scheme theoretic closure of
\Omega_{+}\cap U
in
U
is equal to
U
and as
\eta
was already a nilpotent closed immersion, it is an isomorphism proving (a).

∎

Since
\Vin_{\Omega}
is qc open in
\Vin_{G}
, the subset

(A.1.5)

\Vin_{0}=(G\times G)(k)\cdot\Vin_{\Omega}\subset\Vin_{G}

is an increasing union of qc open subsets.
The action map

G\times G\times\Vin_{0}\rightarrow\Vin_{G}

factors through
\Vin_{0}
, because it factors on
k
-points and
\Vin_{0}
is open.
Thus
\Vin_{0}
is
G\times G
-stable.
We have

\overline{T}_{\Delta}\subset\Vin_{0},

because the coefficient in the highest weight vector of

\omega_{i}(t)\rho_{\omega_{i}}(t^{-1})
is 1.
We consider the group ind-scheme

\Stab_{G\times G}(\sigma)=\{(g_{1},g_{2},t)\in G\times G\times\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}\,;\,g_{1}\sigma(t)g_{2}^{-1}=\sigma(t)\}.

It is closed in
G\times G\times\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}
, which is
viewed as a group ind-scheme over
\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}
.
The section
\sigma
in (
A.1.3
) factors through
\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}\rightarrow\Vin_{0}.

Proposition A.1.4
.

The action map
G\times G\times\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}\rightarrow\Vin_{0}
on the section
\sigma
yields an isomorphism of étale sheaves over
\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}

[(G\times G\times\mathbb{A}_{\Delta})/\Stab_{G\times G}(\sigma)]\cong\Vin_{0}

This isomorphism also holds Zariski locally.

Proof.

By Proposition
A.1.3
, over
\Vin_{\Omega}
we have a section and
\Vin_{0}
is covered by translates of
\Vin_{\Omega}
.
∎

Proposition A.1.5
.

The morphism
\Stab_{G\times G}(\sigma)\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}
is formally smooth.

Proof.

By Proposition
A.1.4
, it is enough to prove the formal smoothness of the action map

G\times G\times\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}\rightarrow\Vin_{0}.

The group ind-scheme
G
is formally smooth, due to our assumptions on
k
or
G
.
Thus the source is formally smooth.
The target is also formally smooth by Proposition
A.1.3
.
The morphism has sections Zariski locally by Proposition
A.1.4
.
Hence it is formally smooth.
∎

We want to describe the fibers over
\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}
of this stabilizer group ind-scheme.
For each subset
J\subset\Delta
, let
e_{J}\in\overline{T}_{\Delta}
be the element with coordinates

\alpha_{j}(e_{J})=0
if
j\in J
and
\alpha_{j}(e_{J})=1
otherwise.
Hence, the linear map
\rho_{\omega_{i}}(e_{J})
is the projection from
L(\omega_{i})
to the sum

L(\omega_{i})_{J}
of the weight subspaces whose weights belong to the set
\omega_{i}+\mathbb{Z}\{\alpha_{j}\,;\,j\in J\}
.

Proposition A.1.6
.

For each subset
J\subset\Delta
we have
\Stab_{G\times G}(e_{J})=P_{J}\times_{L_{J}}P_{J}^{-}
.

Proof.

Let
R
be any
k
-algebra
R
.
Let
(g_{1},g_{2})\in G(R)\times G(R)
be such that
g_{1}e_{J}=e_{J}g_{2}
.
For each
i\in I
, let
M(\omega_{i})_{J}
be the
T
-invariant complement subspace of
L(\omega_{i})_{J}
in
L(\omega_{i})
.
We have

•

L(\omega_{i})_{J}
is stable under
\rho_{\omega_{i}}(g_{1})
,

•

M(\omega_{i})_{J}
is stable under
\rho_{\omega_{i}}(g_{2})
,

•

the maps
L(\omega_{i})_{J}\to L(\omega_{i})_{J}\to L(\omega_{i})/M(\omega_{i})_{J}\cong L(\omega_{i})_{J}

given by

\rho_{\omega_{i}}(g_{1})
and
\rho_{\omega_{i}}(g_{2})
coincide.

The proposition follows from the next lemma.

Lemma A.1.7
.

\mathrm{(a)}

\Stab_{G}(L(\omega_{i})_{J})=P_{J}
and
\Stab_{G}(M(\omega_{i})_{J})=P_{J}^{-}
.

\mathrm{(b)}

The kernel of the
P_{J}
-action on
L(\omega_{i})_{J}
is
U_{J}Z(L_{J})
.

\mathrm{(c)}

The kernel of the
P_{J}^{-}
-action on
M(\omega_{i})_{J}
is
U_{J}^{-}Z(L_{J})
.

Proof.

Set
Q_{J}=\Stab_{G}(L(\omega_{i})_{J})
.
We have
P_{J}\subset Q_{J}
.
So
Q_{J}
is a standard parabolic.
By
[
56
, Thm. 5.1.3(g)]
, since
s_{j}
does not
stabilize
L(\omega_{i})_{J}
if
j\notin J
, we have a bijection on
k
-points.
By considering the open cell
U_{J}^{-}P_{J}
,
we obtain the same way as in Lemma
2.3.5
, that it is an isomorphism.
The second claim in (a) is similar.
To prove (b) it suffices to note that
L(\omega_{i})_{J}
and
M(\omega_{i})_{J}
are
L_{J}
-stable.
Hence the claim
reduces to the Levi, for which it is clear.
∎

∎

A.2.
Proof of Proposition
6.4.2

For any positive root
\alpha
, the map

\alpha\circ 2{\check{\rho}}:\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}
extends to a map
\mathbb{A}^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
.
Taking the product over all simple roots, we get a map

(A.2.1)

\phi:\mathbb{A}^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}.

We introduce the hyperbolic monoid

\textstyle{{\mathrm{Hyp}}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\det}
\textstyle{\Vin_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\det}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}_{\Delta}}

Let
{\mathrm{Hyp}}_{0}
denote the base change of
\Vin_{0}
.
The base change yields a section to the map
\det

\sigma^{{\mathrm{Hyp}}}:\mathbb{A}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Hyp}}_{0}.

Lemma A.2.1
.

\mathrm{(a)}

If
t\neq 0
, then
\Stab_{G\times G}(\sigma^{{\mathrm{Hyp}}}(t))=G
.

\mathrm{(b)}

If
t=0
, then
\Stab_{G\times G}(\sigma^{1}(t))=B\times_{T}B^{-}
.

Proof.

Part (a) is obvious.
If
t=0
then
\sigma(\phi(0))=e_{\emptyset}
in
\Vin^{0}_{G}
.
Hence (b) follows from Proposition
2.3.5
.
∎

Proposition
6.4.2
follows from Proposition
A.1.1
and the following.

Proposition A.2.2
.

\mathrm{(a)}

The action map yields an equivalence of étale sheaves over
\mathbb{A}^{1}

[(G\times G\times\mathbb{A}^{1})/\Stab_{G\times G}(\sigma^{1})]\cong{\mathrm{Hyp}}_{0}

The left hand side is open in an ind-affine ind-scheme.

\mathrm{(b)}

We have
\tilde{G}\cong\Stab_{G\times G}(\sigma^{{\mathrm{Hyp}}})
.

Proof.

Claim (a) follows from Proposition
A.1.4
by base change.
To prove (b) we must relate
\tilde{G}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
with the stabilizer of
\sigma^{1}
.
The fibers of
\tilde{G}
and
\Stab_{G\times G}(\sigma^{{\mathrm{Hyp}}})
over any
t\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
are isomorphic by
Proposition
A.2.1
.
The group
\tilde{G}|_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
is the subscheme of
G\times G\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
such that

\phi(t)\cdot g_{1}\cdot\phi(t)^{-1}=g_{2},\quad\forall g_{1},g_{2}\in G.

On the other hand
\Stab_{G\times G}(\sigma^{1})|_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
is defined by the equation

g_{1}\cdot\sigma^{{\mathrm{Hyp}}}(t)\cdot g_{2}^{-1}=\sigma^{{\mathrm{Hyp}}}(t).

By (
A.1.3
) and base change, the element
\sigma^{{\mathrm{Hyp}}}(t)
differs from
\phi(t)^{-1}
by an element of
Z_{+}
.
Thus the ind-schemes are the same over
\mathbb{G}_{m}
.
Since
\Stab_{G\times G}(\sigma^{1})
is closed in
G\times G\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
,
by Lemma
6.4.1
the closed immersion
\tilde{G}\hookrightarrow G\times G\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
factors as an fp closed immersion

i:\tilde{G}\rightarrow\Stab_{G\times G}(\sigma^{{\mathrm{Hyp}}})

which is fiberwise an isomorphism by Lemma
A.2.1
.
By Lemma
6.1.5
and Proposition
A.1.5
, the groups
\tilde{G}
and
\Stab_{G\times G}(\sigma^{1})
are formally smooth over
\mathbb{A}^{1}
.
Thus by Proposition
B.3.4
,
the map
i
is formally smooth.
Thus it is étale.
Since it is a bijective closed immersion, it is an isomorphism.
∎

Appendix B
Deformation theory for prestacks

The goal of this section is to prove Proposition
B.3.4
, from which Propositions
6.4.2
and
A.2.2
follow.

B.1.
Quasi-coherent sheaves

Let
\dAff_{k}
be the category of affine derived
k
-schemes, and

\dPrSt_{k}=\PrSh(\dAff)
be the category of derived
\infty
-prestacks over
k
.
Let
\Ind(\dAff_{k})\subset\dPrSt_{k}
be the full subcategory of ind-objects in
\dAff_{k}

with no condition on transition morphisms.
The Yoneda embedding
[
60
, Prop. 5.1.3.1]
yields a fully faithful functor

\eta:\dAff_{k}\rightarrow\dPrSt_{k}
that factors through a chain of fully faithful functors

(B.1.1)

\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.78477pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.78477pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 125.51988pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 125.51988pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\dPrSt_{k}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.\end{split}

For any
X\in\dAff_{k}
, let
\QCoh(X)
be the stable
\infty
-category
of quasi-coherent sheaves over
X
,
see
[
61
, Def. 1.3.5.8]
.
It is equipped with the standard
t
-structure, see
[
61
, Prop. 1.3.5.21]
.
Let
\QCoh(X)^{\heartsuit}
be its heart.
For each morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
in
\dAff_{k}
, we have a functor

f^{*}:\QCoh(X)\rightarrow\QCoh(Y).

Thus, there are functors

\Pro(\QCoh),\QCoh:\dAff_{k}^{{\mathrm{op}}}\rightarrow\StCat

that we left Kan-extend to get functors

\Pro(\QCoh),\QCoh:\dPrSt_{k}^{{\mathrm{op}}}\rightarrow\StCat.

In particular, for any prestack
\mathcal{X}
and any
X\in\dAff_{k}
we have

\QCoh(\mathcal{X})=\lim_{X\rightarrow\mathcal{X}}\QCoh(X),

By
[
37
, §3.1.5.1]
, the category
\QCoh(\mathcal{X})
is equipped with a
t
-structure such that

\QCoh(\mathcal{X})^{\leq 0}=\{\mathcal{F}\in\QCoh(\mathcal{X})\,;\,x^{*}\mathcal{F}\in\QCoh(S)^{\leq 0}\,,\,\forall\penalty\ x:S\to\mathcal{X}\}.

The positive category
\QCoh(\mathcal{X})^{\geq 0}
does not have such a convenient description.

B.2.
Functor of derivations

We recall the following construction from
[
11
, 2.1.3]
.

Definition B.2.1
.

For each
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
with finite colimits, we consider the functor
\Fact_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}^{\Delta^{1}}\rightarrow\infty
-
\Cat

which associates to a morphism
x\rightarrow y
in
\mathcal{C}
the
\infty
-category of factorizations
x\rightarrow c\rightarrow y
.

For an affine scheme
S=\Spec(R)
and any
k
-module
M
, let

S[M]=\Spec(R\oplus M)

with the multiplication given by

(a,m)\cdot(a^{\prime},m^{\prime})=(aa,am^{\prime}+a^{\prime}m).

We have a factorization
S\hookrightarrow S[M]\rightarrow S
, where the first map is a square zero closed immersion.
We get a morphism of functors
\Aff\rightarrow\infty
-
\Cat
taking
S
to

S[-]:\QCoh(S)^{\heartsuit}\rightarrow\Fact_{\Aff}(\id_{S}).

We abbreviate

[-]:\QCoh_{\Aff}^{\heartsuit}\rightarrow\Fact_{\Aff}(\id_{-}).

By Lurie
[
62
, Const. 25.3.1.1]
, there exists a unique extension of this functor to
\dAff

and
\QCoh^{\leq 0}
that commutes with small colimits

[-]:\QCoh_{\dAff}^{\leq 0}\rightarrow\Fact_{\dAff}(\id_{-}).

We must extend this functor to any prestack.
Applying the
\Pro
functor, we get a natural transformation

[-]:\Pro(\QCoh_{\dAff}^{\leq 0})\rightarrow\Pro(\Fact_{\dAff}(\id_{-}))\cong\eta_{1}^{*}(\Fact_{\Ind(\dAff)}(\id_{-}))\rightarrow\eta^{*}(\Fact_{\dPrSt}(\id_{-})),

with
\eta_{1}^{*}
and
\eta^{*}
the pullback functors induced by restriction to
\dAff
, see (
B.1.1
).
We then apply the left Kan extension
\eta_{!}
.
It is left adjoint to
\eta^{*}
.
Since
\eta
is fully faithful, the counit
\eta_{!}\eta^{*}\rightarrow\id
is an equivalence.
We obtain

(B.2.1)

[-]:\Pro(\QCoh_{\dPrSt}^{\leq 0})\rightarrow\eta_{!}\eta^{*}(\Fact_{\dPrSt}(\id_{-}))\cong\Fact_{\dPrSt}(\id_{-}).

Definition B.2.2
.

For any morphism of
\infty
-prestacks
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
, we define the derivation functor

\Der_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathcal{X},-)=\Map_{\mathcal{X}/./\mathcal{Y}}(\mathcal{X}[-],\mathcal{X}).

\mathrm{(a)}

\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
admits a relative pro-cotangent complex if there is an object

L_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{Y}}\in\Pro(\QCoh(\mathcal{X}))
such that for each
E\in\Pro(\QCoh^{\leq 0}(\mathcal{X}))
we have

\Der_{\mathcal{Y}}(\mathcal{X},E)\cong\Map(E,L_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{Y}}).

\mathrm{(b)}

\mathcal{X}
admits a pro-cotangent complex if
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\Spec(k)
has a relative pro-cotangent complex.

Remark B.2.3
.

\mathrm{(a)}

Even if in the sequel we use non-derived prestacks, to construct the cotangent complex we must consider the derived setting.

\mathrm{(b)}

For a morphism of
\infty
-prestacks
f:\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
with pro-cotangent complexes, functoriality yields a morphism

(B.2.2)

f^{*}L_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow L_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}/\mathbb{Z}}.

Applying
\Map(-,E)
for each
E\in\Pro(\QCoh^{\leq 0}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}))
, the map
f
has a relative pro-cotangent complex

(B.2.3)

L_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}/\mathcal{X}}\cong Cofib(f^{*}L_{\mathcal{X}/\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow L_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}/\mathbb{Z}}).

Example B.2.4
.

\mathrm{(a)}

Let
X
be an ind-algebraic space.
By
[
50
, Prop. 1.2.19]
it has a pro-cotangent complex
L_{X/\mathbb{Z}}
, such that for any
X\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}X_{a}

and any
x\in X(R)
we have

x^{*}L_{X/\mathbb{Z}}\cong\lim x^{*}L_{X_{a}/\mathbb{Z}}.

In particular, we have
L_{X/\mathbb{Z}}\in\Pro(\QCoh(X)^{\leq 0})
.

\mathrm{(b)}

If
X
is a formally smooth ind-scheme of ind-ft and
H
is a group ind-scheme,
then the quotient
[X/H]
admits a pro-cotangent complex by
[
11
, Prop. 2.2.11]
.

In the formally smooth case, we have more constraints on the pro-cotangent complex.

Proposition B.2.5
.

Let
X
be a formally smooth ind-scheme over a base scheme
S
.
For each point
x:\Spec(R)\rightarrow X
we have

\mathrm{(a)}

H_{0}(x^{*}L_{X/S})
is pro-projective,

\mathrm{(b)}

H_{1}(x^{*}L_{X/S})=0
.

Proof.

For (a), by
[
11
, Lem. 5.2.1]
, given a presentation
X\cong\operatornamewithlimits{colim}X_{a}
, it is enough to prove that the functor on
R
-modules

M\mapsto\Hom_{c,R}(x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/S},M):=\operatornamewithlimits{colim}_{a}\Hom_{R}(x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X_{a}/S},M)

is exact. Denote
\Omega=x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/S}
.
The functor is already left exact. For right exactness, if
M\twoheadrightarrow N
and
\phi\in\Hom_{c,A}(\Omega,N)

it amounts to a cofiltered family of diagrams

\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{X_{a}}\oplus M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{X_{a}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{X_{a}}\oplus N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{X_{a}}}

and because
X
is formally smooth, the section
\phi
lifts for
a
big enough.
Part (b) follows from the proof of
[
36
, Prop. 9.4.2]
.
∎

An
\infty
-prestack
\mathcal{X}
with a pro-cotangent complex
L_{\mathcal{X}}
controls the split square zero extension.
We want to control all square zero extensions. To do this we introduce an extra condition.

B.3.
Prestacks with a Deformation theory

Recall the following definition
[
38
, §I, Def. 7.1.2]
.

Definition B.3.1
.

A morphism
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
of
\infty
-prestacks admits a relative deformation theory if

\mathrm{(a)}

\mathcal{X}
is convergent, i.e., for each
S\in\dAff
we have

\Map(S,\mathcal{X})\cong\lim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Map(\tau^{\leq n}S,\mathcal{X})

where
\tau^{\leq n}S
is the
n
-truncation of
S
.

\mathrm{(b)}

\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
admits a relative pro-cotangent complex.

\mathrm{(c)}

\mathcal{X}
is infinitesimally cohesive, see
[
38
, §I, §6]
.

If
\mathcal{Y}=\Spec(\mathbb{Z})
, we say that
\mathcal{X}
admits a deformation theory.

Remark B.3.2
.

\mathrm{(a)}

To be infinitesimally cohesive for a prestack
\mathcal{X}
amounts to say that for each square zero extension
\bar{S}\hookrightarrow S
,
the groupoid
\mathcal{X}(S)
can be described in terms of
\QCoh(\bar{S})
.

\mathrm{(b)}

Each
n
-truncated prestack is convergent.
In the sequel we will only work with classical prestacks.

\mathrm{(c)}

An ind-scheme
X
has a deformation theory by
[
38
, §II, Prop. 1.3.2]
.
By (
B.2.3
), a morphism of ind-schemes

X\rightarrow Y
has a relative deformation theory. The same holds for an ind-algebraic space, because it is infinitesimally cohesive by

[
62
, Rmk. 17.3.1.7]
and we already checked the other conditions.

\mathrm{(d)}

By
[
11
, Prop. 2.2.11]
, étale quotients
[X/H]
for
X
a formally smooth ind-scheme of ind-ft and
H
an group ind-scheme
have a deformation theory.

Now, the main reason for introducing this property is the following, see
[
62
, Rmk. 17.3.1.8]
.

Proposition B.3.3
.

Let
\Spec(\overline{R})\hookrightarrow\Spec(R)
be a square zero extension of affine schemes of ideal
I
.
Let
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be a morphism of
\infty
-prestacks with a relative deformation theory.
For each commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Spec(\overline{R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\eta}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Spec(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}}

the obstruction to lift
\bar{\eta}
belongs to
\Ext_{\Pro(\Mod_{\overline{R}})}^{1}(\bar{\eta}^{*}L_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{Y}},I)
.
If this obstruction vanishes, then the space of liftings is a trivial torsor under

\Hom_{\Pro(\Mod_{\overline{R}})}(\bar{\eta}^{*}L_{\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{Y}},I)
.

Proof.

Let
I=\Ker(R\twoheadrightarrow\overline{R})
.
By
[
86
, Tag. 08US, 07BP]
, we have
\tau_{\geq-1}L_{\overline{R}/R}=M[1]
.
Thus we get a canonical map
s:L_{\overline{R}/R}\rightarrow M[1]
that gives rise to a derivation
s:\overline{R}\rightarrow\overline{R}\oplus I[1]
.
Equivalently, the map
s
is a morphism of rings that splits the projection
\overline{R}\oplus I[1]\rightarrow\overline{R}
,
such that there is a Cartesian diagram

\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{f}}}
\textstyle{\overline{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\overline{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{\overline{R}\oplus I[1]}

where
i
is the inclusion and
f
the obvious map.
Since
f
is cohesive
[
62
, Def. 17.3.7.1]

there is a pullback square

\textstyle{\mathcal{X}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}(\overline{R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}(\overline{R})\times_{\mathcal{Y}(\overline{R})}\mathcal{Y}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}(\overline{R}\oplus I[1])\times_{\mathcal{Y}(\overline{R}\oplus I[1])}\mathcal{Y}(\overline{R})}

If we fix a point
\eta\in\mathcal{X}(\overline{R})
of image
\bar{\eta}\in\mathcal{Y}(\overline{R})
then we get a fiber sequence of spaces

\{\eta\}\times_{\mathcal{X}(\overline{R})}\mathcal{X}(R)\rightarrow\{\bar{\eta}\}\times_{\mathcal{Y}(\overline{R})}\mathcal{Y}(R)\rightarrow\Hom(\bar{\eta}^{*}L_{X/Y},I[1])

and the desired obstruction, as well as the assertion on torsors.
∎

Proposition B.3.4
.

Let
R
be a noetherian ring.
Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be an ft schematic morphism of ind-schemes of ind-ft over
S=\Spec(R)
.
Assume that

\mathrm{(a)}

X
is formally smooth over
S
,

\mathrm{(b)}

the map
f_{s}
is an open immersion for each
s\in S
.

Then
f
is an open embedding.

Proof.

Let
x:\Spec(R^{\prime})\rightarrow X
, we must show that
\tau_{\geq-1}x^{*}L_{X/Y}=0
. The problem being local, we can assume
R^{\prime}
is local of maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{R^{\prime}}
and
R
local of maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
, so that we have
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathfrak{m}_{R^{\prime}}

through the obvious map
R\rightarrow R^{\prime}
.
Since
X
is formally smooth over
S
, by Proposition
B.2.5
,
\tau_{\geq-1}x^{*}L_{X/S}
is pro-projective concentrated in degree zero, thus in
\Pro(\QCoh(\Spec(R^{\prime})))
, we have an exact sequence:

0\rightarrow H_{1}(x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/Y})\rightarrow x^{*}f^{*}\Omega^{1}_{Y/S}\rightarrow x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/S}\rightarrow x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/Y}\rightarrow 0.

Since
X
and
Y
are of ind-ft over
S
, we deduce that

x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/S}
and
x^{*}f^{*}\Omega^{1}_{Y/S}
are
R^{\prime}
-pro-modules of ft,
as well as
H_{1}(x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/Y})
because
R
is noetherian and

x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/Y}
is a
R^{\prime}
-module of finite type because
f
is schematic.
By Nakayama
[
86
, Tag. 00DV]
and because
f_{s}
is an open immersion for each
s\in S
, we already know that
x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/Y}=0
.
Using that
x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/S}
is pro-projective, we obtain that
H_{1}(x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/Y})/\mathfrak{m}=0
.
Thus, again by Nakayama
[
11
, Lem. 5.4.9]
for pro-modules, we have
H_{1}(x^{*}\Omega^{1}_{X/Y})=0
.
We thus obtain that
f
is formally étale.
We deduce that
f
is an open embedding because if we write
Y=\operatornamewithlimits{colim}Y_{a}
and
X=\operatornamewithlimits{colim}X_{a}=Y_{a}\times_{Y}X
, we have that

f_{a}:X_{a}\rightarrow Y_{a}
is étale.
In particular, the map
X_{a}\rightarrow X_{a}\times_{Y_{a}}X_{a}
is flat and,
since it is fiberwise an isomorphism, it is an isomorphism.
In particular, the map
f_{a}
is an étale monomorphism.
By
[
86
, Tag. 025G]
, it is an open embedding.
∎

B.4.
Equivariant deformations

Let
S
be an affine scheme,
G\rightarrow S
a fppf group scheme and
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow S
a formally smooth prestack with a
G
-action over
S
.
Assume that
\mathcal{X}
admits a deformation theory.
Let

\mathcal{X}^{G}=\lim(G\times\mathcal{X}\rightrightarrows\mathcal{X}\times_{S}\mathcal{X})

be the functor of fixed points, where the morphisms are the action map and the diagonal.
Let
i:S_{0}\hookrightarrow S
be a closed subscheme defined by a quasi-coherent sheaf of ideals
\mathcal{I}
of square zero.
Set
\mathcal{X}_{0}=\mathcal{X}\times_{S}S_{0}
and
G_{0}=G\times_{S}S_{0}
.
Let
\epsilon_{0}\in\mathcal{X}^{G}(S_{0})
.
We want to define an obstruction to lift
\epsilon_{0}
to a point of
\mathcal{X}^{G}(S)
. By formal smoothness and Proposition
B.3.3
,
the space of liftings of
\epsilon_{0}
to
\mathcal{X}(S)
is a trivial torsor under the abelian group

\Map_{\mathcal{O}_{S_{0}}}(\epsilon_{0}^{*}L_{\mathcal{X}/S},\mathcal{I}).

Let
L_{0}
be the
\mathcal{O}_{S_{0}}
-module

L_{0}=\Map_{\mathcal{O}_{S_{0}}}(\epsilon_{0}^{*}L_{\mathcal{X}/S},\mathcal{I}).

Since
\epsilon_{0}
is fixed under
G_{0}
, the
\mathcal{O}_{S_{0}}
-module
L_{0}
is indeed a
G_{0}\ltimes\mathcal{O}_{S_{0}}
-module.
Let
\rho_{0}:G_{0}\rightarrow\Aut_{S_{0}}(L_{0})
be the associated representation.

Proposition B.4.1
.

There is a class
c(\epsilon_{0})
in
H^{1}(G,i_{*}L_{0})=H^{1}(G_{0},L_{0})

whose vanishing is equivalent to the existence of a lift of
\epsilon_{0}
to
\mathcal{X}(S)
.

Remark B.4.2
.

Here the cohomology theory considered is Hochschild cohomology see
[
24
, Exp. I, §5.1]
.

Proof.

The argument follows
[
24
, Exp. XII, Lem. 9.4]
that we recall.
The adjunction
H^{1}(G,i_{*}L_{0})=H^{1}(G_{0},L_{0})
is
[
24
, Exp. III, Lem. 1.1.2]
.
We consider the small fppf site on
S
. For any fppf scheme
T\rightarrow S
, we set
G_{T}
and
\mathcal{X}_{T}
the corresponding base change over
T
. Consider
the sheaf
A
on the small fppf site such that for any fppf scheme
T\rightarrow S
, we have

A(T)=\{\text{set of liftings of}\penalty\ (\epsilon_{0})_{T}\penalty\ \text{in}\penalty\ \mathcal{X}(T)\}.

Because formation of
L_{0}
commutes with flat base change, we get that
A(T)
is a torsor under
H_{0}(T,i_{*}L_{0})
. Because
\epsilon_{0}
is fixed and
T

is fppf on
S
,
g\in G(T)
acts by affine automorphisms on
A(T)
compatibly with the action of
G
on
i_{*}L_{0}
, i.e.,

g(a_{T}+v)=g(a_{T})+\rho(g)(v),v\in H^{1}(T,i_{*}L_{0}).

As
G
is fppf, we apply the above discussion
T=G
and
g=\id_{G}\in G(G)
. We obtain an action of the fppf sheaf
G
on
k
.
Let
a
be a lift
\epsilon_{0}
in
\mathcal{X}(S)
, let
g^{\sharp}
be the universal point of
G
, we define
v^{\sharp}\in H^{1}(G,i_{*}L_{0})
by

\rho(g^{\sharp})(v^{\sharp})=g^{\sharp}a_{G}-a_{G}.

For any
S
-scheme
Y
, we set

z(Y):G(Y)\rightarrow H_{0}(Y,i_{*}L_{0}),\quad g\mapsto v^{\sharp}(g).

This defines an 1-cocycle
z\in Z^{1}(G,L)
and a class
c(\epsilon_{0})
in
H^{1}(G,i_{*}L_{0})
that is independent of
a
, see loc. cit. for details.
In particular if
\epsilon_{0}
lifts to
\mathcal{X}^{G}(S)
, we have
c(\epsilon_{0})=0
.
Conversely if
c(\epsilon_{0})=0
, then there exists
w\in H_{0}(S,i_{*}L_{0})
such that
z(Y)(g)=gw_{Y}-w_{Y}

for each
S
-scheme
Y
and
g\in G(Y)
. By applying it to
Y=G
and
g^{\sharp}
, we get that
a-w\in\mathcal{X}^{G}(S)
, as wished.
∎
